\displaystyle=\frac{1}{180(4\pi)^{2}}\left(R_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}R^{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}-R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}+\Box R\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{180(4\pi)^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}C^{2}-\frac{1}{2}E_{4}+\Box R\right)\,.

(2.109)

As we already stated, we notice that adding the counterterm

\Gamma_{\text{ct}}=\frac{1}{180(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{12}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{g}R^{2}

(2.110)

to the effective action would cancel the
\Box R
term in the trace anomaly.

2.4
Holography

CFTs play an important role in the context of the gauge/gravity duality, which is a realisation of the holographic principle.
Amongst these dualities, CFTs are conjectured to be dual to gravitational theories in anti-de Sitter (AdS) spacetime.
The AdS/CFT correpondence is a very active area of research, which has led to insights into a vast area of physics ranging from particle physics to condensed matter systems.
In this thesis, we are interested in possible tests of the AdS/CFT correspondence, which consist in computing observables on both side of the duality to find agreement between the results.
In particular, we will focus on the trace anomaly, which as we saw is a peculiar feature of a CFT, and we will show how to compute it holographically.
Before doing so, we will present the holographic principle and motivate the AdS/CFT correspondence.
A complete and comprehensive introduction to this topic is by far out of the scope of the present section, and can be found in reviews and books such as
[
Aha+00aa
,
DF02aa
,
Cas+14aa
,
AE15aa
,
Nas15aa
]
.
Here we will cover the essential points needed for the computation of the holographic anomaly.

2.4.1
The holographic principle

The holographic principle states that the information content of a quantum theory of gravity in a certain volume of space is encoded at the boundary surface of this volume
[
t H93aa
]
.
Thus, a gravitational theory in a given space should be equivalent to a non-gravitational theory defined at the boundary of this space.
This principle originates from black hole thermodynamics, according to which the entropy of a black hole is proportional to its horizon area, where it is encoded
[
Bek73aa
,
Haw76aa
]
.
The entropy measures the information inside the horizon, which is inaccessible to an exterior observer, thus realising a hologram.

We provide now an intuitive motivation for a gauge/gravity duality, following
[
Cas+14aa
]
.
Consider a QFT in a
d
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime with coordinates
x^{i}
and with a short-distance cutoff
\epsilon
to avoid UV divergences.
We assume that there is no correlation between degrees of freedom at largely separated points.
If we are interested in the physics at large length scales
z\gg\epsilon
we can integrate out the short-distance degrees of freedom and obtain an effective theory at length scale
z
.
Similarly, we would use an effective theory at scale
z^{\prime}\gg z
if we are interested in the physics at even larger scale
z^{\prime}
.
Iterating this procedure defines an RG flow
[
Wil75aa
]
and gives rise to a continuous family of
d
-dimensional effective field theories labelled by the RG scale
z
.
Pictorially, we can think of this continuous family of
d
-dimensional theories as a single theory in a
(d+1)
-dimensional spacetime with coordinates
X^{\mu}=(z,x^{i})
where the additional coordinate is the RG scale
z
, which foliates the
(d+1)
-dimensional spacetime into
d
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime slices.
By construction, the
(d+1)
-dimensional theory should be invariant under reparametrization of the
z
-coordinate, since the physics of the original theory is invariant under reparametrization of the RG scale.
Moreover, the resulting theory does not have more degrees of freedom than the
d
-dimensional theory, and in particular the effective theory at
z=z_{0}
can describe all the physics for
z\geq z_{0}
.
Together with the holographic principle, this means that the
(d+1)
-dimensional theory is a gravitational theory, whose hologram is the original
d
-dimensional non-gravitational theory.

2.4.2
The AdS/CFT correspondence

To realise a concrete example of gauge/gravity duality we need to specify the theories involved.
Consider a
d
-dimensional CFT with flat metric
\eta_{ij}
and assume that it is the hologram of a
(d+1)
-dimensional gravitational theory.
Demanding that the hologram is conformally invariant allows us to reconstruct the gravitational theory to some extent.
Indeed, the most general
(d+1)
-dimensional metric consistent with
d
-dimensional Poincaré symmetry is given by

ds^{2}=\Omega^{2}(u)\left(du^{2}+\eta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}\right)\,,

(2.111)

where
u
is the extra spatial direction and the warp factor
\Omega
can only depend on
u
to have translational symmetries in the
x^{i}
directions.
Moreover, since the
d
-dimensional theory is a CFT, it is also in particular invariant under scaling of the
x^{i}
coordinates
x^{i}\to\lambda x^{i}
.
As we argued in the previously,
u
represents a length scale in the boundary theory, and thus it also has to transform as
u\to\lambda u
under scaling of the
x^{i}
coordinates.
Assuming that the gravity theory describes such a field theory, then the metric (
2.111
) should respect the scaling symmetry
(u,x^{i})\to\lambda(u,x^{i})
and
the warp factor of the
(d+1)
-dimensional theory is thus fixed to be

\Omega(u)=\frac{\ell}{u}

(2.112)

for some constant
\ell
.
The metric (
2.111
) becomes then

ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{u^{2}}\left(du^{2}+\eta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}\right)\,.

(2.113)

This is the metric of a
(d+1)
-dimensional AdS spacetime with radius
\ell
in Poincaré coordinates.
Thus, assuming the gauge/gravity duality, we conclude that a
d
-dimensional CFT, or
\text{CFT}_{d}
, is dual to a gravitational theory formulated in a
(d+1)
-dimensional AdS spacetime, or
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
.

AdS is a maximally symmetric spacetime with negative cosmological constant.
The isometry group of
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
is
SO(d,2)
, the same symmetry group of a
\text{CFT}_{d}
in Minkowski spacetime.
This symmetry matching provides additional support to the conjecture.
Although AdS spacetime has no boundary in the topological sense, the notion of confomal boundary can be defined.
6
6

6

Henceforth we will refer to the conformal boundary simply as ‘boundary’ for short.

Indeed, consider the metric
\hat{\eta}_{MN}=\text{diag}(-1,1,\ldots,1,-1)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d,2}
with coordinates
y^{M}
.
Then
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
can be embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{d,2}
, where it is given by the hypersurface

\hat{\eta}_{MN}y^{M}y^{N}=-\ell^{2}\,.

(2.114)

Consider now solutions of (
2.114
) of the form
\hat{\eta}_{MN}\tilde{y}^{M}\tilde{y}^{N}=-\ell^{2}/R^{2}
.
In the limit
R\to\infty
the hyperboloid becomes
\hat{\eta}_{MN}\tilde{y}^{M}\tilde{y}^{N}=0
, i.e. the light-cone in
\mathbb{R}^{d,2}
.
But this is true even if we scale out
\lambda R
instead of
R
.
Thus, considering the equivalence classes of points which satisfy
\hat{\eta}_{MN}{y}^{M}{y}^{N}=0
, the boundary of
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
is defined as any section of the light-cone in
\mathbb{R}^{d,2}
which is pierced once be each null ray.
It can be verified that the boundary of
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
corresponds to a compactification of
d
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime, and e.g. in Poincaré coordinates (
2.113
) the boundary is at
u=0
.

If we define a new radial coordinate
z=u^{2}
, then the AdS line element becomes

ds^{2}=\ell^{2}\left(\frac{dz^{2}}{4z^{2}}+\frac{1}{z}\eta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}\right)\,,

(2.115)

and the boundary is at
z=0
.
We will refer to these coordinates as the Fefferman-Graham (FG) coordinates.

The discussion so far provided a first hint for the AdS/CFT correspondence.
Another evidence that a QFT admits a dual description as a gravitational theory comes from the large
N
expansion of a non-abelian gauge theory, as suggested by ’t Hooft
[
t H74aa
]
.
We will state here some remarkable results, without providing any derivation.
In particular, we are interested in the vacuum-to-vacuum amplitude of the Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
U(N)
, which can be expanded in
1/N
as

\mathcal{A}_{\text{YM}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}N^{2-2k}f_{k}(\lambda)\,,

(2.116)

where
\lambda
is the ’t Hooft coupling which is related to the gauge coupling
g_{\text{YM}}
and to
N
by

\lambda=g_{\text{YM}}^{2}N\,,

(2.117)

and the
f_{k}(\lambda)
are functions of the ’t Hooft coupling only.
In particular,
f_{k}(\lambda)
includes the contributions of all Feynman diagrams which can be drawn on a two-dimensional compact surface with genus
k
without crossing any lines.
Thus, the expansion in (
2.116
) can be thought of as an expansion in terms of the topology of the two-dimensional surface.
A similar expression arises in the context of string theory (see e.g.
[
BLT13aa
]
).
The worldsheet of a closed string is a two-dimensional compact surface and the string perturbative expansion is again given by a sum over the worldsheet’s topologies.
The vacuum-to-vacuum amplitude in this case can be written as

\mathcal{A}_{\text{string}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2k-2}F_{k}(\alpha^{\prime})\,,

(2.118)

where
g_{s}
is the string coupling and
\alpha^{\prime}
is related to the inverse of the string tension.
The
F_{k}(\alpha^{\prime})
are the contributions to the amplitude of two-dimensional surfaces with genus
k
.
Comparing (
2.116
) with (
2.118
), it is thus tempting to relate

f_{k}(\lambda)\leftrightarrow F_{k}(\alpha^{\prime})\,,

(2.119)

so that the ’t Hooft expansion is interpreted as a closed string expansion with

g_{s}\sim 1/N\,,\quad\quad\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}(\lambda)\,,

(2.120)

providing further motivation for a gauge/gravity duality, in this case between a non-abelian gauge theory and a theory of closed string.
What this argument does not specify is what particular string theory is taking part in the duality.
A specific example of AdS/CFT correspondence is the famous duality between
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills theory and type IIB string theory on an
\text{AdS}_{5}\times S^{5}
background
[
Mal98aa
]
.
In this case, the relations (
2.120
) between the free parameters of the theories take the explicit forms

g_{\text{YM}}^{2}=2\pi g_{s}\,,\quad\quad 2g_{\text{YM}}^{2}N=\ell^{4}/(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}\,,

(2.121)

where
\ell
is the radius of
\text{AdS}_{5}
and of the five-dimensional sphere.

2.4.3
The dictionary

Once established, the AdS/CFT duality becomes a useful tool if it is known how to map one theory to the other.
This set of relations is usually called the holographic dictionary.
A very important entry of the dictionary is the relation between fields in the bulk and operators in the boundary.
In general, given a bulk field
\Phi_{i}
it is possible to solve its equation of motion with prescribed boundary value
\phi_{i}
.
The
\phi_{i}
is then interpreted as the source for an operator
\mathcal{O}_{i}
in the CFT.
This establishes a one-to-one correspondence between bulk fields and boundary operators

\Phi_{i}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{i}\,.

(2.122)

Consider for example a bulk scalar field
\Phi
with mass
m
coupled to a background metric
G_{\mu\nu}
.
7
7

7

When dealing with holographic systems (i.e. in the remaining of this section and in chap.
5
), we use Greek letters for
(d+1)
-dimensional bulk indices, while Latin letters for
d
-dimensional boundary indices.

Its bulk action is

S=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d+1}X\sqrt{G}\left(G^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial_{\nu}\Phi+m^{2}\Phi^{2}\right)\,,

(2.123)

from which we derive the Klein-Gordon equation of motion

\left(\Box_{G}-m^{2}\right)\Phi=0\,,\quad\quad\Box_{G}\Phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{G}}\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{G}\,G^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\Phi\right)\,.

(2.124)

By writing the equation of motion in FG coordinates (
2.115
), then the solution near the boundary
z\to 0
has the following power series form

\Phi(z,x)=z^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{-}}\left(\phi(x)+\ldots\right)+z^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{+}}\left(\varphi(x)+\ldots\right)\,,

(2.125)

where we indicated only the leading terms and
\Delta_{\pm}
are the roots of
(m\ell)^{2}=\Delta(\Delta-d)
.
Explicitly,

\Delta_{\pm}=\frac{d}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\left(m\ell\right)^{2}}\,.

(2.126)

Notice that
\Delta_{+}\geq\Delta_{-}
and
\Delta_{-}=d-\Delta_{+}
.
Defining
\Delta:=\Delta_{+}
, one can identify
\varphi
as the vacuum expectation value of a scalar operator
\mathcal{O}
of conformal dimension
\Delta
in the dual field theory, and
\phi
as its source
[
GKP98aa
,
Wit98aa
]
.
The relation between the source of the boundary operator and the bulk field is then simply

\phi(x)=\lim_{z\to 0}\Phi(z,x)z^{\frac{1}{2}(\Delta-d)}\,.

(2.127)

The roots
\Delta_{\pm}
are real as long as the mass satisfies the Breitenlohner-Freedman bound
(m\ell)^{2}\geq-d^{2}/4

[
BF82aa
]
.
When
\Delta>d
, the dual operator is irrelevant and
m>0
.
For
\Delta=d
, the dual operator is marginal and the bulk field is massless
m=0
.
When the bulk field has a mass that satisfies
0>(m\ell)^{2}\geq-d^{2}/4
, then the dual operator is relevant with
d/2\leq\Delta<d
.
When
\Delta<d/2
the identification of the source and vev of the boundary operator
\mathcal{O}
can be exchanged and we can describe dual operators of dimension
(d-2)/2\leq\Delta<d/2
while still respecting the mass bound
[
KW99aa
]
.

Another important entry in the dictionary is the identification of the bulk and boundary partition functions

Z_{\text{bulk}}(\phi_{i})=Z_{\text{CFT}}(\phi_{i})\,.

(2.128)

Together with the relation between bulk fields and boundary operators, the identification of the partition functions is very powerful since it allows to perform non-trivial tests of the AdS/CFT correspondence.
The first non-trivial tests consisted in the holographic computation of CFT correlation functions (see e.g.
[
Lee+98aa
,
Fre+99aa
]
) and trace anomaly
[
HS98aa
]
.
Here we will focus on the trace anomaly, which we will derive with the help of a particular class of bulk diffeomorphisms which reduce to Weyl tranformations at the boundary, first constructed in
[
Imb+00aa
]
(see also
[
ST00aa
,
ST03aa
,
ST08aab
,
ST08aac
]
).
These diffeomorphisms are called Penrose-Brown-Henneaux (PBH) transformations and we first briefly review them before computing the holographic anomaly, mainly following the presentation in
[
Bro22aa
]
to set our notation.

2.4.4
PBH transformations

Consider a theory of gravity in a
(d+1)
-dimensional spacetime with coordinates
(z,x^{i})
.
It is possible to bring the bulk metric in the FG form

ds^{2}=G_{\mu\nu}dX^{\mu}dX^{\nu}=\ell^{2}\left(\frac{dz^{2}}{4z^{2}}+\frac{1}{z}g_{ij}(z,x)dx^{i}dx^{j}\right)\,,

(2.129)

where
g_{ij}
depends on the bulk coordinates and
g_{ij}(z=0,x)=g^{(0)}_{ij}(x)
is the boundary metric.
For
g^{(0)}_{ij}=\eta_{ij}
we recover the AdS metric (
2.115
) with radius
\ell
,
8
8

8

For simplicity, in the following we will set
\ell=1
, but we will reintroduce it when necessary.

but the bulk metric (
2.129
) also allows for a curved boundary where
g^{(0)}_{ij}
sources the stress tensor of the CFT.

In order to study the Weyl anomaly of the dual CFT, we first study the behaviour of
G_{\mu\nu}
under diffeomorphisms.
Under a general coordinate transformation

X^{\mu}=X^{\prime\mu}+\xi^{\mu}(X^{\prime})\,,

(2.130)

the bulk metric
G_{\mu\nu}
transforms as

\delta G_{\mu\nu}=G_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\rho}+G_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\rho}+\xi^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}G_{\mu\nu}\,,

(2.131)

with
\delta G_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}G_{\mu\nu}=G^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}(z,x)-G_{\mu\nu}(z,x)

and we require that
\xi^{\mu}
is such that (
2.129
) is form invariant under (
2.130
), i.e.
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}G_{zz}=0=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}G_{zi}
.
The solution is given by

\xi^{z}=-2z\sigma(x)\,,\quad\xi^{i}=a^{i}(z,x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{j}\sigma(x)\int_{0}^{z}dz^{\prime}\,g^{ij}(z^{\prime},x)\,,

(2.132)

where
\sigma(x)
is an arbitrary function, the
a^{i}
are infinitesimal and we will work to order
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,a^{i})
.
The lower end of the integration is chosen so that there are no residual diffeomorphisms at the boundary
z=0
.
In other words, the boundary condition
a^{i}(z=0,x)=0
holds.
Then, performing the diffeomorphism (
2.130
) with (
2.132
),
g_{ij}
transforms as

\delta g_{ij}=2\sigma(1-z\partial_{z})g_{ij}+\nabla_{i}a_{j}+\nabla_{j}a_{i}\,,

(2.133)

where indices are lowered with (and derivatives are covariant w.r.t.)
g_{ij}
.
The equations (
2.132
) and (
2.133
) define the PBH transformations.

We show now that this particular class of bulk diffeomorphisms reduces to a Weyl transformation at the boundary.
Indeed, the commutator of two diffeomorphisms (
2.131
) is again a diffeomorphism

[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]G_{\mu\nu}=G_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\hat{\xi}^{\rho}+G_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\hat{\xi}^{\rho}+\hat{\xi}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}G_{\mu\nu}\,,

(2.134)

where
\hat{\xi}^{\rho}
is defined as

\hat{\xi}^{\rho}=\xi^{\sigma}_{1}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\rho}_{2}-\xi^{\sigma}_{2}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\rho}_{1}+\delta_{2}\xi^{\rho}_{1}-\delta_{1}\xi^{\rho}_{2}\,,

(2.135)

and the last two terms are non-vanishing if we allow
\xi^{\mu}
to be field dependent.
If the diffeomorphism is a PBH, then it is possible to derive that
\hat{\xi}^{\mu}=0
, which is called the PBH group property
[
ST08aab
,
FR21aa
]
, so that
[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]G_{\mu\nu}=0
.
Since the PBH transformations do not act on coordinates but on fields, it follows that
[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]g_{ij}=0
and the PBH transformations reduce to a Weyl transformation on the boundary metric.
Indeed, from (
2.133
) at
z=0
it follows that

\delta g^{(0)}_{ij}=2\sigma g^{(0)}_{ij}\,.

(2.136)

Close to the boundary,
g_{ij}
has a FG expansion
9
9

9

If
d
is an even integer, the expansion of the metric contains also logarithmic terms.
Here we work in generic dimensions, and we do not need to include such terms.

[
FG85aa
]

g_{ij}(z,x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}g^{(n)}_{ij}(x)z^{n}\,,

(2.137)

which induces also the following expansion
10
10

10

The label
(n)
indicating the
n
th term in the radial expansion will be always equivalently used as a super- or subscript.

a^{i}(z,x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{i}_{(n)}(x)z^{n}\,.

(2.138)

Using the PBH equation (
2.133
), we can determine the coefficient in the expansion of the metric in terms of covariant tensors built from the boundary metric as follows.
First, we compute the
a_{(n)}
in terms of the
g_{(n)}
, and for the first few terms we find

\displaystyle a^{i}_{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g_{(0)}^{ij}\partial_{j}\sigma\,,

(2.139)

\displaystyle a^{i}_{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}g_{(1)}^{ij}\partial_{j}\sigma\,.

(2.140)

Indices are now lowered (raised) with the (inverse of)
g^{(0)}_{ij}
; curvatures and covariant derivatives will be w.r.t.
g^{(0)}_{ij}
.
11
11

11

We will stress this by adding a label
(0)
on top of curvature tensors and covariant derivatives.

Then, combining the expansions and the
a_{(n)}
into (
2.133
) we find the variation of
g_{(n)}
as

\displaystyle\delta g^{(0)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\,2\sigma g^{(0)}_{ij}\,,

(2.141)

\displaystyle\delta g^{(n)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\,2\sigma(1-n)g^{(n)}_{ij}+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left(g^{(n-m)}_{ik}\partial_{j}a^{k}_{(m)}+g^{(n-m)}_{jk}\partial_{i}a^{k}_{(m)}+a^{k}_{(m)}\partial_{k}g^{(n-m)}_{ij}\right)\,,

(2.142)

and we see that in general
g_{(n)}
contains
2n
derivatives.
Thus, to compute it we make the most general Ansatz for a symmetric and covariant tensor built from
g^{(0)}_{ij}
with two indices and with
2n
derivatives, take its variation according to (
2.141
) and impose that it satisfies the PBH equation (
2.142
).
In this way, for example for the first two terms we find

\displaystyle g^{(1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{d-2}\bigg(\overset{(0)}{R}_{ij}-\frac{1}{2(d-1)}\overset{(0)}{R}g^{(0)}_{ij}\bigg)\,,

(2.143)

\displaystyle g^{(2)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d-4}\Bigg(\frac{1}{8(d-1)}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\overset{(0)}{R}-\frac{1}{4(d-2)}\overset{(0)}{\Box}\overset{(0)}{R}_{ij}+\frac{1}{8(d-2)(d-1)}g_{(0)ij}\overset{(0)}{\Box}\overset{(0)}{R}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{2(d-2)}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{kl}\overset{(0)}{R}_{ikjl}+\frac{d-4}{2(d-2)^{2}}\overset{(0)}{R}_{ik}\overset{(0)}{R}_{j}{}^{k}+\frac{1}{(d-2)^{2}(d-1)}\overset{(0)}{R}_{ij}\overset{(0)}{R}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{4(d-2)^{2}}\overset{(0)}{R}_{kl}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{kl}g^{(0)}_{ij}-\frac{3d}{16(d-2)^{2}(d-1)^{2}}g^{(0)}_{ij}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+c_{1}\,\overset{(0)}{C}{}^{2}g^{(0)}_{ij}+c_{2}\,\overset{(0)}{C}_{iklm}\overset{(0)}{C}_{j}{}^{klm}\,,

(2.144)

where
C_{ijkl}
is the Weyl tensor.
Starting from
g_{(2)}
, the solutions will have free coefficients that are not fixed by the PBH equation.
On the other hand, if one perturbatively solves Einstein’s equations of motion for the metric (
2.129
) using the expansion (
2.137
),
g_{(2)}
is completely determined in generic dimensions.
The free coefficients in the PBH solutions are thus fixed on-shell given an action.

2.4.5
Effective boundary action and Weyl anomalies

Consider now an action

S=\int_{M}d^{d+1}X\sqrt{G}f(R(G))\,,

(2.145)

where
f
is a local function of the curvature and its covariant derivatives and we require that
f(R)
is such that the equations of motion are solved by
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
in order to have a CFT at the boundary.
Under a bulk diffeomorphism, the action
S
is invariant up to a boundary term

\delta S=\int_{M}d^{d+1}X\,\partial_{\mu}\left(\xi^{\mu}\mathcal{L}\right)\,,\quad\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{G}f(R(G))\,,

(2.146)

from which we read the transformation
\delta\mathcal{L}=\partial_{\mu}\left(\xi^{\mu}\mathcal{L}\right)
. If the diffeomorphism is a PBH, then one can show that
[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]\mathcal{L}=0
upon using the PBH group property
[
ST08aab
]
.
Taking the FG form for the bulk metric, then the FG expansion (
2.137
) induces a power series expansion for
\mathcal{L}
as well

\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{g_{(0)}}z^{-d/2-1}\mathcal{L}_{g}\,,\quad\mathcal{L}_{g}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}^{(n)}_{g}(x)z^{n}\,.

(2.147)

Then, by virtue of
[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]\mathcal{L}=0
, it is possible to show that
\mathcal{L}_{g}
satisfies a WZ condition

\int_{\partial M}d^{d}x\sqrt{g_{(0)}}\left(\sigma_{2}(x)\delta_{1}\mathcal{L}_{g}-\sigma_{1}(x)\delta_{2}\mathcal{L}_{g}\right)=0\,,

(2.148)

which means that
\mathcal{L}_{g}
is a candidate for the anomaly of the boundary CFT.
To make the connection precise, from (
2.146
) we have

\delta S=\int_{\partial M}d^{d}x\,\xi^{z}\mathcal{L}|_{z=0}=-2\int_{\partial M}d^{d}x\,z\,\sigma\,\mathcal{L}|_{z=0}

(2.149)

restricting the diffeomorphism to a PBH.
Using the holographic dictionary, we interpret the variation of the bulk action as the variation of the generating functional of the CFT correlators, so that the finite piece in (
2.149
) gives the holographic Weyl anomaly.
12
12

12

Divergent terms can be cancelled with the addition of counterterms to the bulk action, as first shown for the Einstein-Hilbert action in
[
HS98aa
]
and then further elaborated upon and proven for a generic action in
[
HSS01aa
,
PS05aab
,
PS05aac
,
ABR07aa
]
.

In particular, recall that the Weyl variation of the effective action produces the anomalous trace of the stress tensor and comparing (
2.67
) with (
2.149
) we find the relation

g^{(0)}_{ij}\langle T^{ij}\rangle=-2\mathcal{L}_{g}^{d/2}\,,

(2.150)

which means that
\mathcal{L}^{(n)}_{g}(x)
gives the trace anomaly of the
d=2n
dimensional CFT.

Consider now as an example

2\kappa^{2}f(R(G))=\Lambda-R(G)+\gamma\left(R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\right)\left(G\right)\,,\quad\Lambda=-d(d-1)-2d(d-3)\gamma\,,

(2.151)

where
\gamma
is a dimensionless parameter and
\Lambda
is such that
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
with radius
\ell=1
is a solution of the equations of motion.
13
13

13

For simplicity, in the following we will take
2\kappa^{2}=16\pi G^{(d+1)}_{N}=1
but reintroduce it when necessary.

By writing the action in the FG coordinates (
2.129
) we find (a prime denotes a derivative w.r.t.
z
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g}

\displaystyle=d(1+4\gamma)+\frac{1}{2}z(1+4\gamma)\left[2(1-d)g^{ij}g^{\prime}_{ij}-R(g)\right]+\frac{1}{2}z^{2}\big[4(1+4\gamma)g^{ij}g^{\prime\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(3-4(d-5)\gamma)g^{ik}g^{jl}g^{\prime}_{ij}g^{\prime}_{kl}+(1+8\gamma)\left(g^{ij}g^{\prime}_{ij}\right)^{2}+\gamma(R_{ijkl}R^{ijkl})(g)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+8\gamma g^{\prime}_{ij}R^{ij}(g)\big]+\gamma z^{3}\big[4g^{im}g^{jn}g^{kl}g^{\prime}_{ik}g^{\prime}_{mn}g^{\prime}_{jl}-4g^{ij}g^{kl}g^{mn}g^{\prime}_{ij}g^{\prime}_{km}g^{\prime}_{ln}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-2g^{\prime}_{ij}g^{\prime}_{kl}R^{ikjl}(g)-4g^{im}g^{jn}\nabla_{j}g^{\prime}_{ik}\nabla^{k}g^{\prime}_{mn}+4g^{im}g^{jn}\nabla_{k}g^{\prime}_{ij}\nabla^{k}g^{\prime}_{mn}\big]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\gamma z^{4}\big[g^{im}g^{jn}g^{kp}g^{lq}g^{\prime}_{ik}g^{\prime}_{mn}g^{\prime}_{jl}g^{\prime}_{pq}+(g^{ik}g^{jl}g^{\prime}_{ij}g^{\prime}_{kl})^{2}+8g^{ik}g^{jl}g^{\prime\prime}_{ij}g^{\prime\prime}_{kl}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-8g^{im}g^{jn}g^{kl}g^{\prime}_{ik}g^{\prime}_{mn}g^{\prime\prime}_{jl}\big]\,,

(2.152)

and expanding the metric according to (
2.137
), we identify for instance the terms

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{g}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(1+4\gamma)\overset{(0)}{R}+\frac{1}{2}\left(2-d\right)(1+4\gamma)g^{(1)i}{}_{i}\,,

(2.153)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+12\gamma)\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{ij}g^{(1)}{}_{ij}-\frac{1}{4}(1+4\gamma)\overset{(0)}{R}g^{(1)i}{}_{i}+\frac{3}{2}(4-d)(1+4\gamma)g^{(2)i}{}_{i}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(\frac{1}{8}(8-3d)+\frac{3}{2}\gamma(4-d)\right)g^{(1)i}{}_{i}g^{(1)j}{}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}(1+4\gamma)\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}g^{(1)ij}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(\frac{1}{4}(3d-10)+\gamma(5d-14)\right)g^{(1)}{}_{ij}g^{(1)ij}+\frac{1}{2}(1+4\gamma)\overset{(0)}{\Box}g^{(1)i}{}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma\overset{(0)}{R}_{ijkl}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{ijkl}\,.

(2.154)

On the PBH solutions (
2.143
), (
2.144
) we find the trace anomaly in
d=2,4
respectively:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{g}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(1+4\gamma)\overset{(0)}{R}=-\frac{1}{2}a\overset{(0)}{E}{}_{2}\,,

(2.155)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{g}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}(1+12\gamma)\overset{(0)}{R}{}_{ij}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{ij}+\frac{1}{24}(1+8\gamma)\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma\overset{(0)}{R}_{ijkl}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{ijkl}=-\frac{1}{16}\left(c\,\overset{(0)}{C}{}^{2}-a\,\overset{(0)}{E}{}_{4}\right)

(2.156)

with
c=1-4\gamma
and
a=1+4\gamma
.

{E}{}_{2n}
is the Euler density in
d=2n
, and explicitly
{E}{}_{4}
is as in (
2.72
) while
{C}{}^{2}
as in (
2.73
) here with curvature w.r.t. the boundary metric.
Following the classification of
[
DS93aa
]
, we notice that in
d=2
the holographic computation yields an anomaly which is entirely type A, while in
d=4
there is also a type B as expected from the CFT analysis.
Moreover, for
\gamma=0
we have
a=c
, while in the presence of the quadratic term in the curvature in (
2.151
) then
a-c\neq 0
.
It can be verified that including
R^{2}
and
R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}
terms in the action would also change the values of
a
and
c
, but not their difference
[
BLT13aa
]
, so that (
2.151
) produces the minimal bulk action which allows to distinguish between type A and B anomalies in the pure gravity case.
Having
a\neq c
will be useful in chap.
5
when we include scalar fields in the bulk.
As a final comment, notice that
g^{(n)}_{ij}
does not contribute to
\mathcal{L}^{(n)}_{g}
in
d=2n

[
ST03aa
,
ST08aab
,
Mia14aa
]
.

Consider the case
\gamma=0
and
d=4
.
Then, the action that follows from (
2.151
) corresponds to the action of type IIB supergravity on
\text{AdS}_{5}\times S^{5}
after integration over the five-dimensional sphere and setting to zero all fields except for the metric.
The five-dimensional Newton constant can be expressed as

G^{(5)}_{N}=\frac{G^{(10)}_{N}}{\text{Vol}(S^{5})}\quad\text{with}\quad 16\pi G^{(10)}_{N}=(2\pi)^{7}g_{s}^{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{4}\,.

(2.157)

The dual CFT is
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills with gauge group
SU(N)
and we can check that the holographic computation for the anomaly reproduces the anomaly of this CFT.
Indeed, the trace anomaly computed from holography reads

g^{(0)}_{ij}\langle T^{ij}\rangle=\frac{\ell^{3}}{64\pi G_{N}^{(5)}}\left(\overset{(0)}{R}{}_{ij}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{ij}-\frac{1}{3}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{2}\right)=\frac{N^{2}}{32\pi^{2}}\left(\overset{(0)}{R}{}_{ij}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{ij}-\frac{1}{3}\overset{(0)}{R}{}^{2}\right)\,,

(2.158)

where we reintroduced the
2\kappa^{2}
factor and the dependence on the AdS radius
\ell
; the second equality follows from (
2.157
) and (
2.121
).
This coincides with the trace anomaly of
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills with gauge group
SU(N)
at large
N
and thus provides a test for the AdS/CFT correspondence.

In chap.
5
, we will modify the PBH transformations by including a scalar field in the bulk, focusing on the case in which the scalar field sources an integer-dimensional irrelevant operator on the boundary.
The trace anomaly of a CFT with integer-dimensional irrelevant operators is computed in
[
ST19aa
]
.
We will compute it again from holography and thus provide an additional test of the AdS/CFT correspondence.

Kapitel
3
Correlators and quantum measures of descendant states

In sec.
2.2.5
we stated that correlation functions of descendant fields in two
-
dimensional CFTs can be written as a differential operator acting on the correlator of the respective primary fields.
As an example, we showed the case of the correlator of primary fields with one descendant field, and argued that computing the correlator with more than one descendant is a more laborious task.
We now come back to that issue and in sec.
3.1
we derive a recursive formula valid in a two-dimensional CFT to calculate correlation functions of an arbitrary number of descendants, whose result will depend on the correlator of the respective primaries.
In sec.
3.2
we review some entanglement and distinguishability measures between quantum states, in particular the Rényi entropy, trace square distance and sandwiched Rényi divergence, that are related to correlation function of states.
With our recursive formula, we will then be able to compute these measures for descendant states.
In sec.
3.3
we show some results for descendants of the vacuum; since these are valid in all theories with a unique vacuum, we call them universal.
In sec.
3.4
we focus on descendants of non-vacuum primary fields; these are theory dependent, and we compute the quantum measures in the critical Ising model and the three-state Potts model.
As an application of our results, we will provide a test of the conjectured Rényi Quantum Null Energy Condition (QNEC).
More details and expressions are presented in app.
A
; there, we also report a Mathematica code that implements the recursive formula.
The content of the present chapter was first published in
[
BB21aa
]
, whose presentation we closely follow.

3.1
Computing correlators of descendant fields on the plane

Recall from sec.
2.2
that descendant states in two-dimensional CFTs are generated by acting with the Virasoro generators
L_{-n}
with
n>0
on the primary states.
Since the state-operator correspondence holds, we can assign a field to every state, and for descendant states we write the states and corresponding fields as

|{\Delta,\{(m_{i},n_{i})\}}\rangle=\prod_{i}L^{n_{i}}_{-m_{i}}|{\Delta}\rangle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad f_{|{\Delta,\{(m_{i},n_{i})\}}\rangle}=\prod_{i}\hat{L}_{-m_{i}}^{n_{i}}f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}\,,

(3.1)

where
|{\Delta}\rangle
is a primary state with conformal dimension
\Delta=h+\bar{h}
, with holomorphic and anti-holomorphic conformal weights
h
and
\bar{h}
, and with

\hat{L}_{-m}g(w):=\oint_{\gamma_{w}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{(z-w)^{m-1}}T(z)g(w)

(3.2)

for any field
g
;
\gamma_{w}
is a closed path surrounding
w
.
\hat{L}_{-m}g(w)
is the
m
th ‘expansion coefficient’ in the OPE of the stress tensor
T
with the field
g
.
A similar construction holds for the anti-holomorphic copy of the Virasoro generators.
In what follows we will focus on spinless states
h-\bar{h}=0

Recall also that we denote the field dual to
f_{|{s}\rangle}(z,\bar{z})
by

f_{\langle{s}|}(\bar{z},z):=\left(f_{|{s}\rangle}(z,\bar{z})\right)^{\dagger}\,.

(3.3)

The duality structure of the Hilbert space is fixed by the definitions
L_{-n}^{\dagger}=L_{n}
and
\langle{\Delta}|\Delta^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}}
, and has to be recovered from the two-point function of the corresponding fields at coincident points

\langle{s}|s^{\prime}\rangle\equiv\lim_{z\to w}\left\langle f_{\langle{s}|}(\bar{z},z)f_{|{s^{\prime}}\rangle}(w,\bar{w})\right\rangle\,.

(3.4)

To achieve this one chooses radial quantization around the second insertion point
w
and defines the dual field
f_{\langle{s}|}(\bar{z},z)
as the outcome of the transformation
G(z)=\frac{1}{z-w}+w
of the field
f_{|{s}\rangle}(z,\bar{z})
at the unit circle surrounding
w
.
With the help of the transformation rules that we define in the following sec.
3.1.1
, we can write

f_{\langle{s}|}(\bar{z},z)=f_{\Gamma_{G}|{s}\rangle}\left(\frac{1}{z-w}+w,\frac{1}{\bar{z}-\bar{w}}+\bar{w}\right)\,,

(3.5)

where the action
\Gamma_{G}
on the local Hilber space takes the simple form

\Gamma_{G}=\left(-\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}\right)^{L_{0}}\left(-\frac{1}{(\bar{z}-\bar{w})^{2}}\right)^{\bar{L}_{0}}\exp\left(\frac{L_{1}}{w-z}+\frac{\bar{L}_{1}}{\bar{w}-\bar{z}}\right)\,.

(3.6)

In what follows we will use radial quantization around the origin of the complex plane, i.e. we will choose
w=0
. Note, that (
3.5
) gives (
3.4
) up to a phase factor
(-1)^{h-\bar{h}}
, which will not be important in our analysis.

3.1.1
Transformation of states and fields

The transformation rule for arbitrary holomorphic fields was first presented in
[
Gab94aa
]
. We will, however, use the (equivalent) method introduced in
[
FB04aa
]
.

There is a natural action
M(G)
of a conformal transformation
G
on any Virasoro module and, hence, on the full space of states. For a field
f_{|{s}\rangle}(w)
we need to know how the transformation acts locally around
w
and transform the field accordingly. It works as follows.

Consider a conformal transformation
G
and choose local coordinates around the insertion point
w
and the point
G(w)
. The induced local coordinate change can be written as
\mathfrak{g}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}z^{k}
, where
z
are the local coordinates around
w
that are mapped to the local coordinates
\mathfrak{g}(z)
around
G(w)
. Now solve the equation

v_{0}\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}v_{j}t^{j+1}\partial_{t}\right)t=\mathfrak{g}(t)

(3.7)

for the coefficients
v_{j}
order by order in
t
. The local action of
G
on the module is then given by
M(G):=\exp\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}v_{j}L_{j}\right)v_{0}^{-L_{0}}
. The inverse, that we will rather use, is then given by

\Gamma:=M(G)^{-1}=v_{0}^{L_{0}}\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}v_{j}L_{j}\right)\,,

(3.8)

such that we can write

f_{|{s}\rangle}(G(w))=f_{|{s^{\prime}}\rangle=\Gamma|{s}\rangle}(w)\,.

(3.9)

Note that for a descendant at level
k
we only need the coefficients
v_{j}
up to
j=k
. A Mathematica code to obtain the relation between the coefficients
v_{j}
and
a_{k}
is given in appendix
A.1.1
.

3.1.2
The recursive formula

We will be interested in computing correlation functions

\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}f_{|{s_{i}}\rangle}(z_{i})\rangle\,,

(3.10)

where
|{s_{i}}\rangle
are some descendant states.

To get a handle on them we use Ward identities in a particular way. Therefore, consider a meromorphic function
\rho(z)
that has singularities at most at
z\in\left\{z_{i}\right\}\cup\{0,\infty\}
, where
z_{i}
are the insertion points.
Let us make the particular choice

\rho(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(z-z_{i})^{a_{i}}

(3.11)

for
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, which is in particular regular at
0
. Now, consider the integral identity

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\oint_{\gamma_{z_{i}}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\rho(z)\left\langle T(z)g_{i}(z_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}g_{j}(z_{j})\right\rangle=-\oint_{\gamma_{\infty}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\rho(z)\left\langle T(z)\prod_{j=1}^{N}g_{j}(z_{j})\right\rangle\,,

(3.12)

where
g_{j}
are arbitrary fields, e.g. descendant fields. The latter identity simply follows from deforming the integral contour accordingly. The r.h.s. vanishes for
\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\leq 2
. Next, we consider the functions

\rho_{i}(z):=\prod_{j\neq i}(z-z_{j})^{a_{j}}=\frac{\rho(z)}{(z-z_{i})^{a_{i}}}

(3.13)

for which we need the expansion around
z_{i}
,

\rho_{i}(z)\equiv\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{(n)}\,(z-z_{i})^{n}\,.

(3.14)

Note, that the expansion coefficients
\rho_{i}^{(n)}
are some rational expressions that depend on all
z_{j}\neq z_{i}
and
a_{j}
.
Now, using the definition of
\hat{L}_{m}
in (
3.2
), and the latter expansion we obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{(n)}\left\langle\left(\hat{L}_{a_{i}+n-1}g_{i}(z_{i})\right)\prod_{j\neq i}g_{j}(z_{j})\right\rangle=0\,

(3.15)

for
\sum a_{i}\leq 2
. Note that, even if not written explicitly, the sums over
n
do always terminate for descendant fields
g_{i}
. Note further that these relations among correlation functions depend on the choice of
a_{i}
but the correlators that can be computed from these relations are unique.

One very immediate choice is
a_{i}=1-m
and
a_{j\neq i}=0
which gives the relation

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\hat{L}_{-m}g_{i}(z_{i})\right)\prod_{j\neq i}g_{j}(z_{j})\right\rangle=-\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{n=0}^{\text{lvl}(g_{j})+1}\rho_{j}^{(n)}\left\langle\left(\hat{L}_{n-1}g_{j}(z_{j})\right)\prod_{k\neq j}g_{k}(z_{k})\right\rangle

(3.16)

with

\rho_{j}^{(n)}=(-1)^{n}\binom{n+m-2}{n}(z_{j}-z_{i})^{1-m-n}\,.

(3.17)

For
m>1
we see that the total level of each correlator on the r.h.s., i.e. the sum over all levels of fields appearing in the correlation functions, is lower than the one on the l.h.s. of the equation. We, hence, can express correlation functions of higher total level by correlators of lower total level. One way of computing correlation functions of descendants is using the above formula recursively until there are only
L_{-1}
left. These simply act as derivative operators on the respective primary.

The Mathematica code that uses above equation recursively and computes arbitrary correlation functions of vacuum descendants is given in appendix
A.2.1
. It produces an algebraic expression of the insertion points and the central charge
c
. The Mathematica code to compute correlation function for descendants of generic primary fields is given in appendix
A.2.2
. It produces a derivative operator that acts on the respective primary correlator, which in general is theory dependent.

3.2
Review of some quantum measures in CFT

We want to consider an isolated quantum system living on a circle of length
L
whose (low-energy) physics is governed by a (1+1)-dimensional effective field theory. We assume that at some critical value of its couplings the theory becomes conformal. Then, the system is in some pure state of a (1+1)-dimensional CFT, associated with a density matrix
\rho=|{s}\rangle\langle{s}|
.

Let us further consider a spatial bipartition into a region
A
of size
l<L
and its complement
\overline{A}
. Assume a situation where one has no access to the complement, i.e. all measurements are restricted to the subregion
A
. Our ignorance of the complement means that the state in the region we have access to can be reduced to the density matrix

\rho_{A}={\rm Tr}_{\overline{A}}\rho\,,

(3.18)

where
{\rm Tr}_{\overline{A}}
is the partial trace over the degrees of freedom of the complement.

Our focus of interest lies in reduced density matrices that originate from descendant states of the full system. In particular, we want to study their entanglement and measures of distinguishability between them.

3.2.1
Entanglement measure: Rényi entropy

The
n
th Rényi entropy
[
Ren12aa
,
NC10aa
]
is defined as

S_{n}(A)=\frac{1}{1-n}\log{\rm Tr}_{A}\rho_{A}^{n}\,.

(3.19)

For
n\to 1
it converges to the (von Neumann) entanglement entropy
S(A)=-{\rm Tr}\rho_{A}\log\rho_{A}
which is the most common entanglement measure
[
NC10aa
]
. However, particularly in field theories, there exist alluring analytical tools that make it much easier to compute Rényi entropies for
n>1
than the entanglement entropy. Additionally, many key properties of the entanglement entropy, such as the proportionality of ground state entanglement to the central charge in critical systems and the area law of gapped states, hold for Rényi entropies too. In principle, the knowledge of the Rényi entropy for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
allows to determine all eigenvalues of the reduced density matrix
\rho_{A}
.

In the present case, the full system can be described by a CFT on the Euclidean spacetime manifold of an infinite cylinder for which we choose complex coordinates
u=x+i\tau
with
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and
x+L\equiv x\in\left(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\right]
. The variable
\tau
is regarded as the time coordinate and
x
is the spatial coordinate. As subsystem
A
we choose the spatial interval
\left(-\frac{l}{2},\frac{l}{2}\right)
. In two-dimensional CFTs, the trace over the
n
th power of the reduced density matrix
\rho_{A}={\rm Tr}_{\overline{A}}|{s}\rangle\langle{s}|
is equivalent to a
2n
-point function on the so-called replica manifold which is given by
n
copies of the cylinder glued together cyclically across branch cuts along the subsystem
A
at
\tau=0

[
HLW94aa
,
CC09aa
]
. The exponential map
z(u)=\exp\left(2\pi iu/L\right)
maps the latter manifold to the
n
-sheeted plane
\Sigma_{n}
, where the branch cut now extends between
\exp\left(\pm i\pi\frac{l}{L}\right)
. The
2n
fields are those that correspond to the state
|{s}\rangle
and its dual
\langle{s}|
, where one of each is inserted at the origin of each sheet:

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{A}\rho_{A}^{n}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{n}\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}f_{\langle{s}|}(0_{k})f_{|{s}\rangle}(0_{k})\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{n}}

(3.20)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{n}\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}f_{\Gamma_{-1/z}|{s}\rangle}(\infty_{k})f_{|{s}\rangle}(0_{k})\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{n}}\,.

(3.21)

The constant
\mathcal{N}_{n}=Z(\Sigma_{n})/Z(\mathbb{C})^{n}=\left(\frac{L}{\pi a}\sin\left(\frac{\pi l}{L}\right)\right)^{\frac{c}{3}\left(n-\frac{1}{n}\right)}
,
Z
being the partition function on the respective manifold, ensures the normalization
{\rm Tr}_{A}\rho_{A}=1
, with some UV regulator
a
(for example some lattice spacing). In the second line we use the definition of the dual state.

One way to compute the above correlation function is to use a uniformization map from
\Sigma_{n}
to the complex plane. It is given by composing a Möbius transformation with the
n
th root,

w(z)=\left(\frac{ze^{-i\pi\frac{l}{L}}-1}{z-e^{-i\pi\frac{l}{L}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{n}}\,.

(3.22)

The
2n
fields are mapped to the insertion points

\displaystyle w(0_{k})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{i\pi l}{nL}+\frac{2\pi i(k-1)}{n}\right)

(3.23)

\displaystyle w(\infty_{k})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{i\pi l}{nL}+\frac{2\pi i(k-1)}{n}\right)

on the unite circle, and the fields have to transform as described in section
3.1.1
. The change of local coordinates is given in
A.1.2
. The local action is denoted by
\Gamma_{w(z)}\equiv\Gamma_{k,l}
and for the dual fields we get
\Gamma_{w(1/z)}=\Gamma_{w(z)}\Gamma_{1/z}\equiv\Gamma_{k,-l}
.

Putting all together we see that computing the
n
th Rényi entropy is basically equivalent to computing a
2n
-point function of particularly transformed fields:

\displaystyle e^{(1-n)S_{n}(A)}={\rm Tr}_{A}\rho_{A}^{n}\equiv\mathcal{N}_{n}\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}f_{\Gamma_{k,l}|{s}\rangle}\left(w(0_{k})\right)f_{\Gamma_{k,-l}|{s}\rangle}\left(w(\infty_{k})\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=:\mathcal{N}_{n}F_{|{s}\rangle}^{(n)}\,.

(3.24)

See also
[
Pál14aa
,
TOP16aa
]
for derivations of the latter formula. Other computations of the entanglement entropy of excited states (not necessarily descendants) can also be found in
[
ABS11aa
,
BAS12aa
,
Mos12aa
,
Bha+13aa
,
Tad+13aa
]
.

3.2.2
Distance measures

Distance and other similarity measures between density matrices provide quantitative methods to evaluate how distinguishable they are, where distinguishability refers to the outcome of generic measurements in the different states. There is not a single best measure and not even agreement upon criteria to evaluate different distance measures. Most of them are designed such that they provide the space of (not necessarily pure) states with some additional structure that ideally allows to draw some physically relevant conclusions about the system under consideration. In case of reduced density matrices, distance measures quantify how distinguishable they are by measurements confined to the subregion
A
.

We want to consider two of these measurements for reduced density matrices in two-dimensional CFT, namely the sandwiched Rényi divergence (which is a generalization of the relative entropy) and the trace square distance. Let us denote the reduced density matrices as
\rho_{i}={\rm Tr}_{\overline{A}}|{s_{i}}\rangle\langle{s_{i}}|
, with
\rho_{0}\equiv{\rm Tr}_{\overline{A}}|{0}\rangle\langle{0}|
the reduce density matrix of the vacuum.

Relative entropy

The relative entropy between two reduced density matrices
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
is given by

S(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})={\rm Tr}(\rho_{1}\log\rho_{1})-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{1}\log\rho_{2})\,.

(3.25)

It is free from UV divergences, positive definite and one of the most commonly used distance measures in quantum information, particularly because several other important quantum information quantities are special cases of it, e.g. the quantum mutual information and quantum conditional entropy. The relative entropy also shows to be useful in high energy application when e.g. coupling theories to (semiclassical) gravity. It allows a precise formulation of the Bekenstein bound
[
Cas08aa
]
, a proof of the generalized second law
[
Wal10aa
,
Wal12aa
]
and the quantum Bousso bound
[
Bou+14aa
,
Bou+15aa
]
. It also appears in the context of holography where it can be used to formulate important bulk energy conditions (see e.g.
[
Lin+15aa
,
Las+15aa
,
LV16aa
]
).

However, as in the case of the entanglement entropy there exist no direct analytical tools to compute the relative entropy in generic two-dimensional CFTs. There exist several Rényi type generalisations (see e.g.
[
Las14aa
,
Las16aa
]
) that are more straight forward to compute. We here want to focus on a quite common one called the sandwiched Rényi divergence.

Sandwiched Rényi divergence

The sandwiched Rényi divergence (SRD) between two density matrices
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
is given by

\mathcal{S}_{n}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=\frac{1}{n-1}\log{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{1}^{\frac{1-n}{2n}}\,\rho_{2}\,\rho_{1}^{\frac{1-n}{2n}}\right)^{n}\,.

(3.26)

It is a possible one-parameter generalization of the relative entropy (
3.25
), with the parameter
n\in[\frac{1}{2},\infty)
and
S(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\equiv\mathcal{S}_{n\to 1}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
. The SRD by itself has been shown to enjoy important properties of a measure of distinguishability of quantum states. It is, in particular, positive for all states, unitarily invariant, and decreases under tracing out degrees of freedom
[
Mül+13aa
,
WWY14aa
,
FL13aa
,
Bei13aa
]
.

Due to the negative fractional power of
\rho_{1}
, there is no general method known to compute the SRD for arbitrary states in a CFT. However, if
\rho_{1}
is the reduced density matrix of the theory’s vacuum, then there is a technique introduced in
[
Las19aa
]
to express it in terms of correlation functions. We now briefly review it, and refer to
[
Las19aa
]
for more details.

Let us remind that the reduced density matrix for a sub-system on the cylinder is represented by a sheet of the complex plane with a brunch cut along some fraction of the unit circle with the respective operator insertions at the origin and at infinity of that sheet. In case of the vacuum the corresponding operator is the identity and, hence, we regard it as no operator insertion. Multiplication of reduced density matrices is represented by gluing them along the branch cut. Now, let us consider the Möbius transformation

w(z)=\frac{ze^{-i\pi\frac{l}{L}}-1}{z-e^{-i\pi\frac{l}{L}}}\,,

(3.27)

which maps the two insertions points
0
and
\infty
of a sheet to
e^{\pm\frac{i\pi l}{L}}
and the cut to the negative real axis on every sheet. Now, the reduced density operators can be regarded as operators acting on states defined on the negative real axis by rotating them by
2\pi
and exciting them by locally acting with the respective operators at
e^{\pm\frac{i\pi l}{L}}
. In case of the vacuum reduced density matrix this now allows to define fractional powers by rotating by a fractional angle and even negative powers by rotating by negative angles, which basically means removing a portion of the previous sheet. The latter is, however, only possible if no operator insertion is removed. In the present case, the negative power
\frac{1-n}{2n}
corresponds to an angle
-\pi+\frac{\pi}{n}
. Hence, this construction only makes sense for
\frac{l}{L}<\frac{1}{n}
.
1
1

1

In
[
MRS21aa
]
the interested reader can find arguments why this is not simply an artifact of the CFT construction but holds generally when one assumes that the state is prepared from a Euclidean path integral.
If this requirement holds then
\rho_{0}^{\frac{1-n}{2n}}\rho_{2}\rho_{0}^{\frac{1-n}{2n}}
can be interpreted as a part of the complex plane between angles
\pm\frac{\pi}{n}
with operator insertions at angles
\pm\frac{\pi l}{L}
. This procedure is pictorially presented in figure
3.1
. Finally, taking the cyclic trace of
n
copies of it means gluing
n
of these regions onto each other which results in a
2n
-point function on the complex plane

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{|{s}\rangle}:={\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{0}^{\frac{1-n}{2n}}\rho_{2}\rho_{0}^{\frac{1-n}{2n}}\right)^{n}=\left\langle\prod\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}f_{\Gamma_{k,l}|{s}\rangle}\left(e^{\frac{i\pi l}{L}+\frac{2\pi ik}{n}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{k,-l}|{s}\rangle}\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{L}+\frac{2\pi ik}{n}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

(3.28)

where, in contrast to the previous and following section,
\Gamma_{k,l}
is the local action of the above Möbius transformation
w(z)
followed by a rotation
e^{\frac{2\pi ik}{n}}
to obtain the correct gluing. As before, for the dual field one has to consider
w(1/z)
which is done by replacing
l\to-l
.

-\frac{l}{2}
\frac{l}{2}
(i)
e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{L}}
e^{\frac{i\pi l}{L}}
(ii)
\frac{2\pi}{n}
(iii)

Abbildung
3.1:
Pictorial representation of the geometric setting for the SRD. (i) The reduced density matrix is represented by the sheet with respective operator insertions (red crosses) at 0 and
\infty
. (ii) A Möbius transformation maps the insertion points to
e^{\pm\frac{i\pi l}{L}}
and the branch cut to the negative real line. (iii) The multiplication by negative fractional powers of the reduced vacuum states is given cutting out respective parts of the sheet.

Let us highlight the connection between rather formal definitions of distinguishability measures and physical features of a theory. The latter is the Quantum Null Energy Condition (QNEC) which follows from the so-called Quantum Focusing Conjecture
[
Bou+16aab
]
. The QNEC gives a lower bound on the stress-energy tensor in a relativistic quantum field theory that depends on the second variation of entanglement of a subregion. The QNEC can also be formulated solely in terms of quantum information theoretical quantities and has been shown to be equivalent to positivity of the second variation of relative entropies
[
LLS18aa
]
. After the QNEC has been proven in free and holographic theories
[
Bou+16aac
,
KL16aa
,
ML20aa
]
, it has since been shown to hold quite generally in the context of Tomita-Takesaki modular theory
[
Bal+19aa
,
CF20aa
]
. A generalized version of QNEC has been suggested in
[
Las19aa
]
and proven to be true in free theories in dimensions larger than two
[
MRS21aa
]
. This generalization may be called ‘Rényi QNEC’ and is formulated as the positivity of the second variation of sandwiched Rényi entropies. The diagonal part of the second variation is simply given by the second derivative of the SRD with respect to the subsystem size. Hence, the Rényi QNEC can only be true in a theory if any SRD is a convex function of the subsystem size. We will explicitly check if this is true in our results in secs.
3.3
and
3.4
.

Trace square distance

The trace square distance (TSD) between two reduced density matrices is given by

T^{(2)}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}):=\frac{{\rm Tr}|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|^{2}}{{\rm Tr}\rho_{0}^{2}}=\frac{{\rm Tr}\rho_{1}^{2}+{\rm Tr}\rho_{2}^{2}-2{\rm Tr}\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{{\rm Tr}\rho_{0}^{2}}\,,

(3.29)

where the factor
{\rm Tr}\rho_{0}^{2}
in particular removes any UV divergences and allows to directly express the TSD in terms of four-point functions on the two-sheeted surface
\Sigma_{2}
(see also
[
SU16aa
]
),

\displaystyle T^{(2)}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{\langle{1}|}(0_{1})f_{|{1}\rangle}(0_{1})f_{\langle{1}|}(0_{2})f_{|{1}\rangle}(0_{2})\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{2}}+\left\langle f_{\langle{2}|}(0_{1})f_{|{2}\rangle}(0_{1})f_{\langle{2}|}(0_{2})f_{|{2}\rangle}(0_{2})\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle-2\left\langle f_{\langle{1}|}(0_{1})f_{|{1}\rangle}(0_{1})f_{\langle{2}|}(0_{2})f_{|{2}\rangle}(0_{2})\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{2}}\,.

(3.30)

Using the uniformization map (
3.22
) with
n=2
we can express it in terms of four-point functions on the complex plane,

\displaystyle T^{(2)}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{\Gamma_{1,-l}|{1}\rangle}\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{1,l}|{1}\rangle}\left(e^{\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{2,-l}|{1}\rangle}\left(-e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{2,l}|{1}\rangle}\left(-e^{\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle+\left\langle f_{\Gamma_{1,-l}|{2}\rangle}\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{1,l}|{2}\rangle}\left(e^{\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{2,-l}|{2}\rangle}\left(-e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{2,l}|{2}\rangle}\left(-e^{\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle-2\left\langle f_{\Gamma_{1,-l}|{1}\rangle}\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{1,l}|{1}\rangle}\left(e^{\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{2,-l}|{2}\rangle}\left(-e^{-\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)f_{\Gamma_{2,l}|{2}\rangle}\left(-e^{\frac{i\pi l}{2L}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

(3.31)

The trace square distance is manifestly positive and has the great advantage that we can compute it directly in terms of four-point correlators, i.e. there is no need to consider higher-sheeted replica manifolds and we do not need to take any analytic continuations.
Different trace distances between (not necessarily descendant) states in two-dimensional CFTs have been considered in e.g.
[
SU16aa
,
ZRC19aab
,
ZRC19aac
]
.

3.3
Universal results from the vacuum representation

Most physically interesting conformal field theories contain a unique vacuum that naturally corresponds to the identity field. For the vacuum all the above correlation functions to compute the quantum measures become trivial. However, the theories also contain the whole vacuum representation which for example consists of the state
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
that corresponds to the holomorphic part of the stress tensor,
T(z)
. Correlation functions of vacuum descendant fields generically depend on the central charge of the theory and can be computed explicitly using the Ward identities (
3.15
) or (
3.16
) recursively.
Since all quantities discussed in section
3.2
can be expressed in terms of correlators, we can in principle compute all of them as closed form expressions, too.
However, since we use computer algebra to perform the transformations and compute the correlation functions, computer resources are the biggest limiting factor.
Here we mostly present results for all descendants up to conformal weight five, and in some cases up to descendants at level 10. In particular, we want to check how the measures depend on the conformal weights of the states and if states at the same conformal weight can be regarded as similar.

3.3.1
Rényi entanglement entropy

Only for the first few excited states in the identity tower, the expressions (
3.24
) to compute the second Rényi entanglement entropy are compact enough to display them explicitly. In case of the first descendant
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
, i.e. the state that corresponds to the stress tensor, we get

\displaystyle F^{(2)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}\sin^{8}(\pi x)}{1024}+\frac{c\sin^{4}(\pi x)(\cos(2\pi x)+7)^{2}}{1024}+\frac{\sin^{4}(\pi x)(\cos(2\pi x)+7)}{16c}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{16200\cos(2\pi x)-228\cos(4\pi x)+120\cos(6\pi x)+\cos(8\pi x)+16675}{32768}\,,

(3.32)

where we defined
x=l/L
. The results for the states
L_{-n}|{0}\rangle
with
n=3,4,5
are given in
A.3.1
. The results here agree with those in
[
TOP16aa
]
when present.

One important case is the limit of small subsystem size, i.e. when
x\ll 1
. In this limit to leading order any of the above
2n
-point functions (
3.24
) decouple into
n

2
-point functions. This is because the operator product of a field and its conjugate includes the identity. Then, in the limit
x\to 0
the respective identity block dominates and takes the form of a product of
n
2-point functions. Those two point functions are, however, given by the transition amplitude from the state to its dual on the
k
th sheet that decouples in the limit
x\to 0
from all other sheets. The latter is simply given by the squared norm of the state, i.e. it gives one for normalized states. Hence, we can write

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0}F_{|{s}\rangle}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{k=1}^{n}\lim_{x\to 0}\langle f_{\Gamma_{k,l}|{s}\rangle}\left(w(0_{k})\right)f_{\Gamma_{k,-l}|{s}\rangle}\left(w(\infty_{k})\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{k=1}^{n}\langle{s}|s\rangle=1\,.

(3.34)

Hence, to order
x^{0}
the descendant does not play any role at all. For the next-to-leading-order result there are expectations from primary excitations and the change of the entanglement entropy computed from holography. E.g. in
[
Bha+13aa
]
it is shown that the change should be proportional to the excitation energy and, in particular, should be independent from
c
. Expanding the results (
3.3.1
), (
A.3.1
)-(
A.3.1
) we obtain

F_{L_{-n}|{0}\rangle}^{(2)}=1-\frac{n}{2}\left(\pi x\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(x^{4}\right)\,,\quad\quad\text{for}\penalty\ n=2,3,4,5\,,

(3.35)

which is in agreement with all above expectations.

In figure
3.2
we show the results for
F^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}
for the states
|{s}\rangle=L_{-n}|{0}\rangle
,
n=2,3,4,5
. The first observation is that at large
c
the correlator shows an oscillating behaviour with oscillation period proportional to
1/n
. In fact, we can see this also from the explicit results (
3.3.1
), (
A.3.1
)-(
A.3.1
) where at large central charge the term proportional to
c^{2}
dominates.
Note that the correlator
F^{(n)}
can become larger than one at large central charge and, hence, its contribution to the Rényi entropy
S^{(n)}
can get negative. For example, in case of
n=2
and
|{s}\rangle=L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
this happens at
x=1/2
for
c\gtrsim 18.3745
.

(a)
(b)
(c)
(d)

for (a)

, (b)
|{s}\rangle=L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
, (c)
|{s}\rangle=L_{-4}|{0}\rangle
, (d)
|{s}\rangle=L_{-5}|{0}\rangle
, for several values of the central charge.

The vacuum module is degenerate at conformal weight
h=4
and
h=5
. In addition to the states
L_{-4}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-5}|{0}\rangle
there are the states
L_{-2}^{2}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-3}L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
, respectively. Their correlators
F^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}
are shown in figure
3.3
(a) and (b) for different values of the central charge. Interestingly, although their small subsystem behaviour is given by (
3.35
) and, hence, it is the same as for
L_{-4}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-5}|{0}\rangle
, respectively, their general behaviour is rather different at large central charge. Their oscillation period is not proportional to the conformal weight but proportional to the level of the lowest Virasoro generator appearing in it.

Already these two examples show that the behaviour of the Rényi entropy and, hence, also of the entanglement entropy of descendant states does not only depend on their conformal weight, i.e. the energy of the state, but also significantly on their building structure.
This is particularly true at large central charge, limit in which a CFT is conjectured to be dual to a (semi-)classical gravity theory. It is widely believed that black hole microstates in
\text{AdS}_{3}
correspond to typical high conformal dimension states in the CFT. However, a typical state at conformal dimension
\Delta\gg 1
is a descendant at level
\Delta/c
of a primary with conformal dimension
\tfrac{c-1}{c}\Delta
(see e.g.
[
DKM19aa
]
). This means that a typical state will be a descendant at large but finite central charge
c
. The results we present here show that descendants with the same conformal dimension can in fact show very different behaviour when it comes to the entanglement structure.
It will be interesting to further study the large
c
limit, in particular for non-vacuum descendants, to analyse the holographic effect of these different behaviours.

Finally, in figure
3.3
(c) and (d) we show the correlator
F^{(3)}
for the first two excited states
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
. They show qualitatively the same behaviour as the respective correlators for
n=2
(see figure
3.2
(a) and (b)). However, their dependence on the central charge is stronger and the oscillating behaviour starts at lower
c
. For example,
F^{(3)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle}
is larger than one at
x=1/2
for
c\gtrsim 14.74945
.

(b)
(c)
(d)

F^{(n)}_{|{s}\rangle}
for (a)
n=2
,

, (b)

, (c)
n=3

, and (d)

|{s}\rangle=L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
for several values of the central charge.

The stronger dependence on the central charge for larger
n
is expected. Indeed, we can expand
F^{(n)}_{|{s}\rangle}
as

F^{(n)}_{|{s}\rangle}=\sum_{k=-n+1}^{n}A_{k}^{(n)}c^{k}\,,

(3.36)

where all the dependence on the state
|{s}\rangle
and the relative subsystem size
x=l/L
sits in the coefficients
A_{k}^{(n)}
. The negative powers of
c
originate from the normalization of the state, while positive powers of
c
follow from the Virasoro commutation relations when using the Ward identities. Therefore, at large central charge we get

\left.F^{(n)}_{|{s}\rangle}\right|_{c\gg 1}\simeq A_{n}c^{n}\,.

(3.37)

3.3.2
Sandwiched Rényi divergence

As argued in section
3.2.2
it is possible to express the SRD (
3.26
) for integer parameters
n
in terms of a
2n
-point function
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}
(
3.28
) if
\rho_{1}
is the reduced density matrix of the vacuum.
When
\rho_{2}
is associated to the state
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(2)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\cos(4\pi x)+7)\sec^{8}(\pi x)}{16384c}[-512\cos(4\pi x)+128\cos(8\pi x)+384

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+c(847\cos(4\pi x)-22\cos(8\pi x)+\cos(12\pi x)+1222)]\,,

(3.38)

where
x=l/L<1/2
. More expressions for different descendants can be found in appendix
A.3.2
.

Again we first want to draw attention to the small subsystem limit. The results for the second SRD between the reduced vacuum state and all states up to conformal weight five show the small subsystem behaviour

\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}=\frac{2h_{s}^{2}}{c}\pi^{4}x^{4}+\frac{2h_{s}^{2}}{3c}\pi^{6}x^{6}+\mathcal{O}(x^{8})\,.

(3.39)

The small subsystem behaviour only depends on the central charge and the conformal weight of the respective state and is independent of the specific structure of the state.

In case of
n=2
, the SRD diverges at
x=1/2
. In the limit
x\to 1/2
find the behaviour

\mathcal{F}^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}=\exp\left(\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}\right)=\frac{A_{|{s}\rangle}}{\pi^{4h_{s}}\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{4h_{s}}}+\ldots\,,

(3.40)

where the coefficient
A_{|{s}\rangle}
depends on the specifics of the state. For states of the form
L_{-n}|{0}\rangle
up to
n=10
it takes the form

A_{L_{-n}|{0}\rangle}=\binom{2n-1}{n-2}^{2}\,.

(3.41)

In figure
3.4
we show the SRD for the first six excited states.
All of them show a plateau at small values of
x
that increases for larger
c
and shrinks for higher energy. This is expected from the asymptotic result (
3.39
).
It is interesting to compare the second SRD of the states
L_{-2}^{2}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-3}L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
to that of the states
L_{-4}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-5}|{0}\rangle
with the same conformal weight.
The plots show that, although in the asymptotic regimes
x\to 0
and
x\to 1/2
the second SRD behaves similarly for degenerate states, it looks quite different for intermediate regimes of
x
.
In particular, the SRD of the states
L_{-2}^{2}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-3}L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
is more sensible to the central charge.
This shows again that descendant states at the same conformal dimension can behave quite differently, especially at large central charge.

for different values of the central charge

.

3.5:
The second SRD between the reduced groundstate and the states
L_{-n}|{0}\rangle
,
n=2,3,4,5,10
at
c=1/1000
, showing non-convex behaviour and thus violation of the Rényi QNEC conjecture.

In all plots so far the second SRD is a convex function of the relative subsystem size
x=l/L
. However, in cases of small central charge it is not, i.e. there are regions of
x
with
\partial^{2}S^{(2)}/\partial x^{2}<0
. For example, in case of
|{s}\rangle=L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
the second SRD is not convex for
c\lesssim 0.1098
. This shows that there are examples where the generalized version of the QNEC is not true. However, CFTs with central charges smaller than 1/2 are quite unusual. They cannot be part of the ADE classifiation of rational, unitary, modular invariant CFTs
[
CIZ87aa
]
but could e.g. be logarithmic
[
NR21aa
]
.
In figure
3.5
we show the second SRD for states
L_{-n}|{0}\rangle
with
n=2,3,4,5,10
and
c=1/1000
to illustrate its non-convexity for all these states.

3.3.3
Trace squared distance

Again only the expressions for the first few excited states are compact enough to display them explicitly. For example, the TSD between the vacuum and the state
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
is given by

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}\sin^{8}(\pi x)}{1024}-\frac{1}{512}c\sin^{6}(\pi x)(\cos(2\pi x)+15)+\frac{\sin^{4}(\pi x)(\cos(2\pi x)+7)}{16c}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{32768}(-32768\cos(\pi x)+8008\cos(2\pi x)-228\cos(4\pi x)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+120\cos(6\pi x)+\cos(8\pi x)+24867)\,,

(3.42)

where we use the abbreviation
x={l}/{L}
again.
Some other explicit expressions can be found in appendix
A.3.3
.

In the limit
x\to 0
the reduced states have no support and, hence, must be trivial. Consequently, the trace square distance vanishes in this limit independently of the original states we choose. We checked the leading order in
x\ll 1
for all states up to conformal weight five and we find the behaviour

T^{(2)}_{s_{1},s_{2}}=\frac{2+c}{16c}(h_{1}-h_{2})^{2}\pi^{4}x^{4}+\mathcal{O}(x^{6})\,.

(3.43)

We can see that to leading order,
x^{4}
, the TSD depends on the central charge and the difference in conformal weight of the two states. We also see that for large central charge the dependence on
c
is negligible.

In case of
h_{1}-h_{2}=0
the TSD starts at order
x^{8}
for small
x
. We e.g. obtain

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{L_{-2}^{2}|{0}\rangle,L_{-4}|{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2c+1)^{2}\left(25c^{3}+420c^{2}+2444c+4752\right)\pi^{8}x^{8}}{1600c(c+8)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(x^{10})\,,

(3.44)

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{L_{-3}L_{-2}|{0}\rangle,L_{-5}|{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{9c\left(25c^{3}+420c^{2}+2444c+4752\right)\pi^{8}x^{8}}{1024(c+6)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(x^{10})\,.

(3.45)

Albeit one common factor, the latter expressions do not seem to show a straightforward dependence on the states. They also show that the large
c
behaviour is more subtle because the
x^{8}
coefficient diverges as
c\to\infty
.

In the opposite limit
x\to 1
the TSD can be computed easily because the states become pure. One obtains

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 1}T^{(2)}_{|{s_{1}}\rangle,|{s_{2}}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm Tr}(|{s_{1}}\rangle\langle{s_{1}}|^{2})+{\rm Tr}(|{s_{2}}\rangle\langle{s_{2}}|^{2})-2{\rm Tr}(|{s_{1}}\rangle\langle{s_{1}}||{s_{2}}\rangle\langle{s_{2}}|)}{{\rm Tr}(|{0}\rangle\langle{0}|^{2})}

(3.46)

\displaystyle=2\left(1-|\langle{s_{1}}|s_{2}\rangle|^{2}\right)=:\mathcal{T}\,.

(3.47)

We can see that
0\leq\lim_{x\to 1}T^{(2)}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\leq 2
where we get the first equal sign if and only if
s_{1}=s_{2}
and the second one if and only if the two states are orthogonal to each other.

The explicit results up to conformal weight five show that the expansion around
x=1
is given by

T^{(2)}_{|{s_{1}}\rangle,|{s_{2}}\rangle}=\mathcal{T}\left(1-\frac{h_{1}+h_{2}}{4}\pi^{2}(x-1)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left((x-1)^{4}\right)\right)\,.

(3.48)

We can see that the behaviour of the TSD close to
x=1
depends on the sum of conformal weights
h_{1}+h_{2}
.
This is in contrast to the small
x
behaviour that depends on the difference.
Let us, for example, consider the second TSD between the vacuum and
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
in (
3.3.3
) and the second TSD between the vacuum and
L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
in (
A.3.3
).
From the difference of conformal weight we get

T^{(2)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}(x)<T^{(2)}_{L_{-3}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}(x)

(3.49)

for small
x
.
However, from the sum of conformal weights we obtain

T^{(2)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}(x)>T^{(2)}_{L_{-3}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}(x)

(3.50)

for
x
close to one.
We immediately can conclude that there must be an odd number of values
x\in(0,1)
, which in particular means at least one, with

T^{(2)}_{L_{-2}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}(x)=T^{(2)}_{L_{-3}|{0}\rangle,|{0}\rangle}(x)\,.

(3.51)

We also visualise some of the results. In figure
3.6
we show the second TSD between the vacuum
|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-n}|{0}\rangle
for
n=2,3,4
, and between the first two excited states in the vacuum module,
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
. In all these examples only for small enough
c
the TSD is a monotonic function for
x\in[0,1]
. At larger
c
the function starts to meander and can get even bigger than 2, the maximum value of the TSD between pure states. However, the reduced density matrices are not pure and it is not a contradiction that the TSD behaves like this. Still, it is hard to interpret the quantity as a meaningful measure of distinguishability for large values of
c
at intermediate values of the relative subsystem size
x=l/L
.

(a)
(b)
(c)
(d)

Abbildung
3.6:
The TSD between the reduced states of (a) the vacuum and
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
, (b) the vacuum and
L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
, (c) the vacuum and
L_{-4}|{0}\rangle
, and (d) the states
L_{-3}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
for different values of the central charge
c
.

(a)
(b)

Abbildung
3.7:
The TSD between the degenerate states at (a)
h_{s}=4
, i.e.
L_{-4}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-2}^{2}|{0}\rangle
, and (b)
h_{s}=5
, i.e.
L_{-5}|{0}\rangle
and
L_{-3}L_{-2}|{0}\rangle
, for different values of
c
.

In figure
3.7
we show the TSD between the two degenerate states at conformal dimension
h_{s}=4
and
h_{s}=5
for different values of
c
. As expected from the results (
3.44
) and (
3.45
) we see a quite large flat region at small
x
. At
x\to 1
they converge to the TSD of the respective pure states. In the regions in between they show qualitatively the same behaviour as the other TSDs. For larger central charge they start to meander and at very large
c
the term proportional to
c^{2}
dominates, so that the TSD becomes very large, too.

3.4
Theory dependent results

For non-vacuum descendant states, using the relation (
3.16
) recursively allows to express the correlation function of holomorphic descendants
f_{|{s_{i}}\rangle}
as a differential operator acting on the correlation function of the respective primary fields

\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}f_{|{s_{i}}\rangle}(z_{i})\rangle=\mathcal{D}\,\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}(z_{i})\rangle\,.

(3.52)

In general,
\mathcal{D}
depends on the central charge of the CFT, on the conformal weights of the primary fields, and on the insertion points. As a differential operator it acts on the holomorphic coordinates.
In appendix
A.2.2
we provide a code to compute it analytically in Mathematica.
If the correlation function of the primaries is known, then it is possible to compute the descendant correlator through (
3.52
).

The correlators in (
3.24
), (
3.28
), and (
3.2.2
) can be written as linear combinations of correlation functions of descendants with coefficients that follow from the respective conformal transformations, i.e. the uniformization map (
3.22
) in case of the Rényi entropy and the TSD, and the usual Möbius transformations (
3.27
) followed by a rotation in case of the SRD. Combining this with (
3.52
) we can write each of the correlators as

D\bar{D}\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}(z_{i})\rangle\,,

(3.53)

with differential operators
D,\bar{D}
. Since we only consider holomorphic descendants,
\bar{D}
is simply given by the anti-holomorphic part of the transformation of primaries,

\bar{D}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\bar{v}_{0;(k,l)}^{\bar{h}_{k}}\bar{v}_{0;(k,-l)}^{\bar{h}_{k}}\,.

(3.54)

To give an example, for the correlator of the
n
th Rényi entropy (
3.24
) we simply get
\bar{D}=\sin^{4\bar{h}}(\pi l/L)
from the uniformization map.

In the following sections we explicitly show the expressions of the differential operators
D\bar{D}
for the simplest descendant state
L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle
. We will then consider results for higher-level descendants by acting with the operators on particular primary four-point functions in two specific CFTs, the Ising model and the three-state Potts model.

The Ising model is one of the simplest CFTs
[
DMS97aa
]
.
It is a unitary minimal model with central charge
c=1/2
and contains three primary operators: the identity, the energy density
\varepsilon
and the spin field
\sigma
, whose holomorphic conformal weights are
0
,
1/2
,
1/16
respectively.
The
2n
-point correlation functions on the plane of the
\varepsilon
and
\sigma
operators are known
[
DMS97aa
]
and, in particular, the four-point correlator of the energy density reads

\left\langle\varepsilon(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\ldots\varepsilon(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\right\rangle=\left|\frac{1}{(z_{12}z_{34})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(z_{13}z_{24})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(z_{23}z_{14})^{2}}\right|\,,

(3.55)

while the four-point correlator of the spin is given by

\left\langle\sigma(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\ldots\sigma(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{|z_{14}z_{23}|^{1/4}}\frac{\sqrt{1+|\eta|+|1-\eta|}}{|\eta|^{1/4}}\,,

(3.56)

where
z_{ij}=z_{i}-z_{j}
and
\eta=z_{12}z_{34}/z_{13}z_{24}
is the cross ratio.
Given these expressions, it is possible to study the Rényi entanglement entropy and the quantum measures for various descendants of
\varepsilon
and
\sigma
.

The three-state Potts model is the unitary minimal model with
c=4/5

[
DMS97aa
]
.
It can be realized as a particular class of the more general
N
-state clock model which enjoys
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry:
for
N=2
one recovers the Ising model, while the case
N=3
is equivalent to the three-state Potts model
[
FK80aa
,
FZ85aa
,
FZ87aa
,
Dot84aa
]
.
Its operator content is richer than that of the Ising model and it includes six spinless primary operators with conformal weight
0
,
2/5
,
7/5
,
3
,
1/15
, and
2/3
.
In particular, the dimensions of the thermal operator
\varepsilon
and the spin field
\sigma
are
2/5
and
1/15
respectively.
Again, a number of correlation functions between operators of the three-states Potts model are known (see e.g.
[
FZ85aa
,
Dot84aa
]
) and, since we will focus on descendants of the energy operator in the following, we provide here the four-point correlation function of the energy density
[
Dot84aa
]
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\varepsilon(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\ldots\varepsilon(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|z_{13}z_{24}|^{8/5}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\eta(1-\eta)|^{8/5}}\left|{}_{2}F_{1}\left(-\tfrac{8}{5},-\tfrac{1}{5};-\tfrac{2}{5};\eta\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{\Gamma\left(-\tfrac{2}{5}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{6}{5}\right)\Gamma\left(\tfrac{13}{5}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\tfrac{12}{5}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(-\tfrac{1}{5}\right)\Gamma\left(-\tfrac{8}{5}\right)}|\eta(1-\eta)|^{6/5}\left|{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{6}{5},\tfrac{13}{5};\tfrac{12}{5};\eta\right)\right|^{2}\Bigg]\,,

(3.57)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}
is the hypergeometric function.

3.4.1
Rényi entanglement entropy

Let us first consider
F_{|{s}\rangle}^{(2)}
with
|{s}\rangle=L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle
.
As discussed above we can write

F_{|{s}\rangle}^{(2)}=\bar{D}^{F^{(2)}}D_{L_{-1}}^{F{(2)}}\,\left\langle f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(e^{-\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(e^{\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(-e^{-\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(-e^{\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\,,

(3.58)

with
\bar{D}_{L_{-1}}^{F^{(2)}}=\sin^{4\bar{h}}(\pi x)
and
D_{L_{-1}}^{F^{(2)}}
can be computed to be

\displaystyle D_{L_{-1}}^{F(2)}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{64}\sin^{4h}(\pi x)\Big[4h^{2}(3\cos(2\pi x)+5)^{2}+\frac{16\sin^{4}(\pi x)}{h^{2}}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}

\displaystyle+he^{-\frac{7}{2}i\pi x}\left(3+e^{2i\pi x}\right)^{2}\left(-2e^{2i\pi x}+3e^{4i\pi x}-1\right)\left(\partial_{2}-\partial_{4}\right)

\displaystyle+he^{-\frac{9}{2}i\pi x}\left(1+3e^{2i\pi x}\right)^{2}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}+e^{4i\pi x}-3\right)\left(\partial_{3}-\partial_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+8\sin^{2}(\pi x)(3\cos(2\pi x)+5)\left(\partial_{1}\partial_{2}+\partial_{3}\partial_{4}-\partial_{2}\partial_{3}-\partial_{1}\partial_{4}\right)

\displaystyle-e^{-3i\pi x}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}+e^{4i\pi x}-3\right)^{2}\partial_{2}\partial_{4}-e^{-5i\pi x}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}-3e^{4i\pi x}+1\right)^{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{h}e^{-\frac{7}{2}i\pi x}\left(-1+e^{2i\pi x}\right)^{3}\left(3+e^{2i\pi x}\right)\left(\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{4}-\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{h}e^{-\frac{9}{2}i\pi x}\left(-1+e^{2i\pi x}\right)^{3}\left(1+3e^{2i\pi x}\right)\left(\partial_{1}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}-\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\right)\Big]\,,

(3.59)

where
\partial_{n}
is the partial differentiation w.r.t. the
n
-th insertion point and
x=l/L
.
Unfortunately, already at level 2 the general expression are too cumbersome to express them here explicitly.

Given the four-point correlation functions (
3.55
), (
3.56
), (
3.4
), we can compute
F_{L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle}^{(2)}
from eq. (
3.58
) for
h=1/2,\,1/16
in the Ising model and
h=2/5
in the three-states Potts model.
We performed the same computations for descendants up to level 3 and show the results in figure
3.8
; some analytic expressions are given in appendix
A.3.1
and
A.3.1
.

Abbildung
3.8:
The correlator
F^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}
for different descendants of
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
and
|{\sigma}\rangle
in the Ising model and
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
in the Potts model.

In the Ising model, there is only one physical state in the module of the energy operator at each level up to level 3.
A consequence is that
F_{L_{-2}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}=F_{L_{-1}^{2}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
, even though
D^{F^{(2)}}_{L_{-2}}\neq D^{F^{(2)}}_{L_{-1}^{2}}
.
The same happens at level 3 for the different descendant states
L_{-3}|{\varepsilon}\rangle
,
L_{-1}^{3}|{\varepsilon}\rangle
and
L_{-2}L_{-1}|{\varepsilon}\rangle
.
2
2

2
