With an abuse of notation, we will henceforth denote the primary states related to the energy and spin fields in the Ising and Potts models with
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
and
|{\sigma}\rangle
, instead of labelling them with their conformal dimensions.

For
\sigma
descendants, again there is only one physical state at level 2 and
F_{L_{-2}|{\sigma}\rangle}^{(2)}=F_{L_{-1}^{2}|{\sigma}\rangle}^{(2)}
, but at level 3 there are two physical states and
L_{-3}|{\sigma}\rangle
,
L_{-1}^{3}|{\sigma}\rangle
and
L_{-2}L_{-1}|{\sigma}\rangle
produce different REEs as shown in figure
3.8
.
Notice that the REEs for the different descendants of
\sigma
at level 3 have a similar behaviour for small values of
x
, but are clearly distinguishable for
x\sim 1/2
.

For descendants states of the energy density of the three-states Potts model there is again only one physical state at level 2 and two physical states at level 3.
Similarly to the case of descendants of
\sigma
in Ising, we found that
F_{L_{-2}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}=F_{L_{-1}^{2}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
but the different descendants that we considered at level 3 produced different REEs, as plotted in figure
3.8
.
Notice that also in Potts the behaviour for small
x
is given by the level and not by the state configuration, while all the curves are distinguishable for
x\sim 1/2
.
In particular,
F_{L_{-1}^{3}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
behaves more like
F_{L_{-1}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
than
F_{L_{-3}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
for
x\sim 1/2
, while the plot of
F_{L_{-2}L_{-1}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
is very similar to
F_{L_{-3}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}
.

If we expand the analytic results for energy descendants in both the Ising and Potts model for small
x
, we find the behaviour

F_{L_{-n}|{\varepsilon}\rangle}^{(2)}=1-\frac{n+2h_{\varepsilon}}{2}(\pi x)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(x^{4})\quad h_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\begin{matrix}1/2&\text{Ising}\\
2/5&\text{Potts}\end{matrix}\right.\,,\quad n=1,2,3\,.

(3.60)

This is in general expected, since for small subsystem size
z_{1}\sim z_{2}
and
z_{3}\sim z_{4}
and to first order the four-point function is
(h=\bar{h}=\Delta/2)

\left\langle f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\frac{1}{|z_{12}z_{34}|^{4h}}\,.

(3.61)

Then, using this correlation function in (
3.58
) as well as in the corresponding equations for higher descendants and taking the small
x
limit we reproduce precisely eq. (
3.60
), which is the clear generalization of eq. (
3.35
) in agreement with
[
ABS11aa
]
.
However, the leading behaviour of
F^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\sigma}\rangle}
is different from the one outlined in (
3.60
).
This happens because in the OPE of two Ising spin operator there is an additional contribution, that is absent in the OPE of two energy operators or subleading in the case of Potts.
Indeed, consider in general the OPE between two primary fields

f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=\frac{1}{|z_{12}|^{4h_{i}}}+\frac{C^{k}_{ii}\,f_{|{\Delta_{k}}\rangle}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})}{|z_{12}|^{4h_{i}-2h_{k}}}+\ldots\,,

(3.62)

where we included the contribution from the lightest primary field
f_{|{\Delta_{k}}\rangle}
in the module of
f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}
.
Then, to this order the four-point function for
z_{1}\sim z_{2}
and
z_{3}\sim z_{4}
becomes

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\ldots f_{|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{|z_{12}z_{34}|^{4h_{i}}}+\frac{(C^{k}_{ii})^{2}}{|z_{12}z_{34}|^{4h_{i}-2h_{k}}}\frac{1}{|z_{24}|^{4h_{k}}}\,,

(3.63)

so that

\displaystyle F_{L_{-n}|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{n+2h_{i}}{2}(\pi x)^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(C^{k}_{ii}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{c(n-1)^{2}+4nh_{i}+2n^{2}(h_{k}-1)h_{k}}{c(n-1)^{2}+4nh_{i}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)^{4h_{k}}+\ldots\,.

(3.64)

The second term is in general a subleading contribution, like in the Potts model
\varepsilon\times\varepsilon=\mathbb{I}+X
with X having dimension
7/5
.
However, due to the fusion rule
\sigma\times\sigma=\mathbb{I}+\varepsilon
in Ising, in this case
h_{k}=1/2
with
C_{\sigma\sigma}^{\varepsilon}=1/2

[
Mus20aa
]
, and we see that the second term in (
3.4.1
) contributes to leading order.
Indeed, eq. (
3.4.1
) correctly predicts the small
x
behaviour of
F^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\sigma}\rangle}
for
n=1,2,3
that we computed and report in app.
A.3.1
.

Some results for the Rényi entanglement entropy in the Ising and three-states Potts models were already considered in
[
Pál14aa
,
TOP16aa
,
Tad+13aa
]
; we checked that our code produces the same analytic expressions studied in these references.

3.4.2
Sandwiched Rényi divergence

Consider now the correlator
\mathcal{F}^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}
related to the SRD as in eq. (
3.28
) with
|{s}\rangle=L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle
.
Then, we find

\mathcal{F}^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}=\bar{D}^{\mathcal{F}(2)}D_{L_{-1}}^{\mathcal{F}(2)}\,\left\langle f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(e^{-i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(e^{i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(-e^{-i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(-e^{i\pi x})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\,.

(3.65)

From the anti-holomorphic part of the conformal transformation we now obtain

\bar{D}^{\mathcal{F}(2)}=2^{4\bar{h}}\sin^{4\bar{h}}(\pi x)

(3.66)

and the differential operator acting on the holomorphic coordinates reads

\displaystyle D_{L_{-1}}^{\mathcal{F}(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{4h}}{h^{2}}e^{-2i\pi x}\sin^{4h}(\pi x)\bigg[4h^{4}e^{2i\pi(h+1)x}\left(e^{-2i\pi x}\right)^{h}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+2h^{3}e^{i\pi x}\left(1-e^{2i\pi x}\right)(\partial_{1}+\partial_{4}-\partial_{2}-\partial_{3})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+h^{2}\left(e^{2i\pi x}-1\right)^{2}(\partial_{1}\partial_{4}+\partial_{2}\partial_{3}-\partial_{1}\partial_{2}-\partial_{1}\partial_{3}-\partial_{2}\partial_{4}-\partial_{3}\partial_{4})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+4ihe^{2i\pi x}\sin^{3}(\pi x)(\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{3}+\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}-\partial_{1}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}-\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{4})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{4}\left(e^{2i\pi x}-1\right)^{4}e^{2i\pi(h-1)x}\left(e^{-2i\pi x}\right)^{h}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}\bigg]\,.

(3.67)

We explicitly study the results for descendants up to level 3. The general expressions for
D
are, however, again too cumbersome to show them here.
With the four-point functions (
3.55
), (
3.56
), (
3.4
) we compute
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{|{s}\rangle}
for the descendants of the energy and spin primary states in Ising and of the energy state in Potts.
The results are plotted in figure
3.9
and some closed expressions are given for descendants of the energy state of Ising in appendix
A.3.2
.

Abbildung
3.9:
The SRD between the reduced groundstate and different descendants of
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
and
|{\sigma}\rangle
in the Ising model and
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
in the Potts model.

As expected, the SRDs start from 0 and diverge at
x=1/2
.
We also see from the plots that for higher-level descendants the SRD grows more rapidly.
In the Ising model degenerate descendants of
\varepsilon
at level 2 and 3 produce the same SRDs, while for degenerate descendants of
\sigma
at level 3 we found three different expressions.
However, the differences between the plotted results are so small that the three curves at level 3 overlap in figure
3.9
.
The same happens for descendants of
\varepsilon
in the Potts model.

Now, let us check the limit of small subsystem size.
Consider the OPE between two primary fields (
h=\bar{h}=\Delta/2
)

f_{\Delta}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})f_{\Delta}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=\frac{1}{|z_{12}|^{4h}}+\frac{2hc^{-1}\,T(z_{2})}{z_{12}^{2h-2}\bar{z}_{12}^{2h}}+\frac{2hc^{-1}\,\bar{T}(\bar{z}_{2})}{z_{12}^{2h}\bar{z}_{12}^{2h-2}}+\ldots\,,

(3.68)

where for now we only included the leading contributions from the vacuum module.
Then, if we insert this OPE in the four-point function for
z_{1}\sim z_{2}
and
z_{3}\sim z_{4}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\ldots f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{|z_{12}z_{34}|^{4h}}+\frac{2h^{2}c^{-1}}{|z_{12}z_{34}|^{4h}}\frac{z_{12}^{2}z_{34}^{2}}{z_{24}^{4}}+\frac{2h^{2}c^{-1}}{|z_{12}z_{34}|^{4h}}\frac{\bar{z}_{12}^{2}\bar{z}_{34}^{2}}{\bar{z}_{24}^{4}}\,.

(3.69)

With this expression we can study the limit
x\to 0
in (
3.65
) and similar expressions for higher-level descendants.
We find

\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle}=\frac{2}{c}\left(n^{2}+2nh+2h^{2}\right)(\pi x)^{4}+\ldots\,.

(3.70)

Expanding our analytic results for descendants of the energy in Ising and Potts for
x\to 0
we found agreement with eq. (
3.70
).
For
\sigma
descendants, however, the leading order contribution to the SRD in the limit
x\to 0
is different.
Indeed, if we think of the OPE as in (
3.62
) with the correlator (
3.63
), then we find the following leading contribution in the SRD for
n=1,2,3

\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle}=\left(C^{k}_{ii}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{c(n-1)^{2}+4nh_{i}+2n^{2}(h_{k}-1)h_{k}}{c(n-1)^{2}+4nh_{i}}\right)^{2}\left(\pi x\right)^{4h_{k}}+\ldots\,.

(3.71)

Since
h_{k}=1/2
for
|{\Delta_{i}}\rangle=|{\sigma}\rangle
in the Ising model, we see that the contribution from the
\varepsilon
channel dominates over the one from the stress tensor in (
3.70
).
We checked that (
3.71
) with
C^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}=1/2
correctly reproduce the
x\to 0
limit of our results.

It is interesting to consider also the opposite limit
x\to 1/2
and see how the SRDs scale with the singularity.
In this case, it is enough to consider the first contribution in the OPE (
3.68
), but making the appropriate changes as with our insertion points
x\to 1/2
means
z_{1}\sim z_{4}
and
z_{2}\sim z_{3}
.
Then, for
n=1,2,3
we find the following expression

\mathcal{F}^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle}=\exp\left(\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle}\right)=\frac{A_{n}}{\pi^{-4(2h+n)}\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-4(2h+n)}}+\ldots\,,

(3.72)

with

A_{n}=(-1)^{8h}\left(\frac{(n-1)(3n-5)(3n-4)\frac{c}{2}+4\left(\frac{6^{n}}{3}-1\right)h+2(n+1)^{2}h^{2}}{c(n-1)^{2}+4nh}\right)^{2}\,.

(3.73)

Notice that for
h\to 0
we recover the same scaling as in (
3.40
).

In all the examples that we considered, the SRD proved to be a convex function of
x
, providing further evidence to the validity of the Rényi QNEC in two dimensions
[
MRS21aa
]
for large enough central charge.

3.4.3
Trace square distance

Consider now the trace square distance between a primary state
|{\Delta}\rangle
and its first descendants
L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle
. Then

T_{L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}^{(2)}=\bar{D}^{T(2)}D_{L_{-1}}^{T(2)}\,\left\langle f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(e^{-\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(e^{\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(-e^{-\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})f_{|{\Delta}\rangle}(-e^{\frac{1}{2}i\pi x})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\,,

(3.74)

where again the differential operator on the anti-holomorphic coordinates is simply given by the transformation factor

\bar{D}^{T(2)}=\sin^{4\bar{h}}(\pi x)\,,

(3.75)

while the differential operator on the holomorphic coordinates is given by:

\displaystyle D_{L_{-1}}^{T(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{64}\sin^{4h}(\pi x)\bigg[4(3h\cos(2\pi x)+5h-4)^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+2e^{-\frac{5}{2}i\pi x}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}-3e^{4i\pi x}+1\right)(3h\cos(2\pi x)+5h-8)\partial_{1}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+2e^{-\frac{3}{2}i\pi x}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}+e^{4i\pi x}-3\right)(3h\cos(2\pi x)+5h-8)\partial_{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+he^{-\frac{9}{2}i\pi x}\left(1+3e^{2i\pi x}\right)^{2}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}+e^{4i\pi x}-3\right)\partial_{3}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-he^{-\frac{7}{2}i\pi x}\left(3+e^{2i\pi x}\right)^{2}\left(-2e^{2i\pi x}+3e^{4i\pi x}-1\right)\partial_{4}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+8\sin^{2}(\pi x)(3\cos(2\pi x)+5)\left(\partial_{3}\partial_{4}-\partial_{2}\partial_{3}-\partial_{1}\partial_{4}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-e^{-3i\pi x}\left(2e^{2i\pi x}+e^{4i\pi x}-3\right)^{2}\partial_{2}\partial_{4}-4e^{-i\pi x}(2i\sin(2\pi x)+\cos(2\pi x)-1)^{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{3}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{8}{h}\sin^{2}(\pi x)(3h\cos(2\pi x)+5h-8)\partial_{1}\partial_{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{16}{h}e^{\frac{i\pi x}{2}}\sin^{3}(\pi x)(\sin(\pi x)+2i\cos(\pi x))\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{16}{h}e^{-\frac{1}{2}i\pi x}\sin^{4}(\pi x)(1-2i\cot(\pi x))\left(\partial_{1}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}-\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\frac{1}{h}e^{-\frac{7}{2}i\pi x}\left(e^{2i\pi x}-1\right)^{3}\left(3+e^{2i\pi x}\right)\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{4}+\frac{16}{h^{2}}\sin^{4}(\pi x)\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\partial_{4}\bigg]\,.

(3.76)

Again, we limit ourselves to display this result, which is the simplest, since for higher descendants the expressions become much more involved.
As in the previous cases, we computed
T^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}
as in (
3.74
) for
n=1,2,3
and for the degenerate states at level 2 and 3.
Then, by using the four-point functions (
3.55
), (
3.56
), and (
3.4
) we obtained analytic expressions for the TSD between the primary state and its descendants for the energy and spin operators in the Ising model and for the energy in the three-states Potts model.
Figure
3.10
shows the plots of the results, while in appendix
A.3.3
and
A.3.3
we provide some explicit expressions.

Abbildung
3.10:
TSD between different descendants and their primary state
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
and
|{\sigma}\rangle
in the Ising model and
|{\varepsilon}\rangle
in the Potts model.

In the Ising model we find that degenerate states of the energy density produce the same TSD w.r.t. the primary state up to level 3. This again is as expected.
For spin descendants instead this is not true at level 3, with
T^{(2)}_{L_{-3}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}\neq T^{(2)}_{L_{-1}^{3}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}\neq T^{(2)}_{L_{-2}L_{-1}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}
.
However, in the small and large subsystem size limits we see that these different expressions have the same behaviour, while they differ the most around
x\sim 1/2
.
In the Potts model, TSDs between degenerate states at level 3 and the energy density are again different, but from the plots we see that the difference is barely visible, and in particular for
x\to 0
and
x\to 1
it is negligible.

If we study the small subsystem size limit, we can generically predict the behaviour of the TSD.
Consider for instance the OPE between two primary states as given by (
3.68
) and the correlator as in (
3.69
).
Then, we find the following behaviour in the limit
x\to 0
for
n=1,2,3

T^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}=\frac{2+c}{16c}n^{2}(\pi x)^{4}+\mathcal{O}(x^{6})

(3.77)

in agreement with the vacuum result in (
3.43
) and with the analytic expressions that we found in Ising and Potts models for energy descendants.
However, for
\sigma
descendants in the Ising model the leading-order contribution as
x\to 0
does not come from the stress tensor but from the energy field
\varepsilon
in the OPE.
Indeed, consider again the OPE as in (
3.62
) with the correlator (
3.63
), then the contribution to the TSD as
x\to 0
for
n=1,2,3
reads

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}=\left(C_{hh}^{h_{k}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{2n^{2}(h_{k}-1)h_{k}}{c(n-1)^{2}+4nh}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)^{4h_{k}}+\ldots\,.

(3.78)

We see that this term dominates over the one one outlined in (
3.77
) for
h_{k}<1
, which is the case for the Ising spin.
We checked that (
3.78
) with
C^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}=1/2
matches the small
x
behaviour of the results for
\sigma
presented in appendix
A.3.3
.

Consider now the large subsystem size limit
x\to 1
.
Then, with our coordinates we have
z_{1}\sim z_{4}
and
z_{2}\sim z_{3}
and by taking the OPE similarly as in (
3.68
) but with appropriate insertion points we find the behaviour

T^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}=2-\left(2h+\frac{n}{2}\right)\pi^{2}(x-1)^{2}+\ldots\,,

(3.79)

which agrees with the
x\to 1
limit of the explicit results we found for descendants of the energy in Ising and Potts.
Again, for
\sigma
descendants we need to take into account the contribution from the lightest field in the OPE.
We then find

T^{(2)}_{L_{-n}|{\Delta}\rangle,|{\Delta}\rangle}=2\left(C^{h_{k}}_{hh}\right)^{2}C_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{4h_{k}}(x-1)^{4h_{k}}

(3.80)

where

\displaystyle C_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}(n-1)^{4}+4cn(n-1)\left(2(n-1)h+(1-2^{n-1})h_{k}\right)}{\left(c(n-1)^{2}+4nh\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{(4n)^{2}h^{2}-(2n)^{3}hh_{k}+2n^{4}(h_{k}-1)^{2}h_{k}^{2}}{\left(c(n-1)^{2}+4nh\right)^{2}}\,.

(3.81)

For
\sigma
in the Ising model
h_{k}=1/2
and we see that the contribution from the
\varepsilon
channel sums up with the leading correction in (
3.80
).
Once this is taken into account, we correctly match the large
x
limit of the
\sigma
expressions in appendix
A.3.3
.

Kapitel
4
Weyl fermions and trace anomalies

In this chapter we study the trace anomalies of Weyl fermions in four dimensions, first in a gauge background and then in a gravitational background.
Our interest is in the Pontryagin term (
2.74
) presented in sec.
2.3.1
, that, as we already stated, has never appeared in the trace anomalies of known CFTs, until its presence in the case of Weyl fermions was first claimed in
[
BGL14aa
]
and then confirmed in
[
BPL15aa
,
Bon+17aa
,
Bon+18aa
]
.
However, an independent analysis could not detect this term
[
BM16aa
]
and claimed for additional studies.
Here we present our contribution to this subject.

In sec.
4.1
we first consider an analogous and simplified example, namely that of a Weyl fermion in a non-abelian gauge background, whose trace anomaly could receive contribution from a parity-odd term proportional to the Chern-Pontryagin density of the gauge background.
To ascertain this possibility, we consider the Bardeen model of Dirac fermions coupled to non-abelian gauge fields
[
Bar69aa
]
and compute all chiral and trace anomalies with the PV regularisation scheme presented in sec.
2.3.2
.
The results for the Weyl fermions are then obtained by taking suitable limits on the background.
We reproduce the already known chiral anomalies to test our scheme, and then compute the trace anomaly, finding no parity-odd term in it.
Our result for the trace anomaly generalises to the non-abelian case the expression found in
[
BB19aab
]
.

In sec.
4.2
we consider the metric-axial-tensor (MAT) background constructed in
[
Bon+17aa
,
Bon+18aa
]
that generalises to curved space the approach used by Bardeen.
We study a Dirac fermion coupled to this background and compute the anomalies again in the PV regularisation scheme.
Suitable limits on the background eventually allow us to obtain the anomalies for the Weyl fermions in a curved spacetime, and again we find that the Pontryagin density does not contribute to the trace anomaly.

We collect in app.
B
our conventions for Dirac and Weyl fermions and more details on the calculations.

The results of this chapter were first published in
[
BB19aac
,
BB20aa
]
, whose presentation we follow.

4.1
Non-abelian gauge background

In this section we compute the chiral and trace anomalies of Weyl fermions coupled to non-abelian gauge fields in four dimensions.
To do so, we consider the Bardeen model
[
Bar69aa
]
, which embeds the Weyl theory into the theory of massless Dirac fermions
\psi
coupled to vector and axial
non-abelian gauge fields,
A_{a}
and
B_{a}
.
This model is classically gauge invariant and conformally invariant. We wish to compute systematically the anomalies.
The chiral anomaly was already studied in
[
Bar69aa
]
and we recompute it to test our methods. The main result will be to obtain the trace anomaly.

4.1.1
Bardeen model

The lagrangian of the Bardeen model is given by

{\cal L}=-\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}\left(\partial_{a}+A_{a}+B_{a}\gamma^{5}\right)\psi=-\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}D_{a}(A,B)\psi=-\overline{\psi}D\!\!\!\!/\,(A,B)\psi\,,

(4.1)

where
D_{a}(A,B)
is the covariant derivative for the gauge group
G\times G
.
We expand the gauge fields on the generators of the gauge group as
A_{a}=-iA_{a}^{\alpha}T^{\alpha}
and
B_{a}=-iB_{a}^{\alpha}T^{\alpha}
.
The components
A_{a}^{\alpha}
and
B_{a}^{\alpha}
are real,
and
T^{\alpha}
denote the hermitian generators in the representation of
G
chosen for
\psi
.
The generators satisfy the Lie algebra
[T^{\alpha},T^{\beta}]=if^{\alpha\beta\gamma}T^{\gamma}
with anti-symmetric structure constants
f^{\alpha\beta\gamma}
.
Taking an appropriate limit on the background, i.e. by setting
A_{a}=B_{a}\to{A_{a}}/{2}
, a chiral projector arises and we are left with the theory of left-hended Weyl fermions, which is the one we are eventually interested in (see app.
B.1
for our conventions on Dirac and Weyl fermions).

Let us first review the classical symmetries. The lagrangian is invariant under the
G\times G
gauge transformations.
Using infinitesimal parameters
\alpha=-i\alpha^{\alpha}T^{\alpha}
and
\beta=-i\beta^{\alpha}T^{\alpha}
, they read

\displaystyle\delta\psi

\displaystyle=-\left(\alpha+\beta\gamma^{5}\right)\psi\,,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\delta\overline{\psi}

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}\left(\alpha-\beta\gamma^{5}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{c}

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha^{T}-\beta^{T}\gamma^{5}\right)\psi_{c}\,,

\displaystyle\delta A_{a}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}\alpha+[A_{a},\alpha]+[B_{a},\beta]\,,

\displaystyle\delta B_{a}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}\beta+[A_{a},\beta]+[B_{a},\alpha]\,,

where
\psi_{c}=C^{-1}\overline{\psi}^{T}
is the charge conjugated spinor.
The transformations of the gauge fields can be written more compactly as

\delta{\cal A}_{a}=\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}+[{\cal A}_{a},\tilde{\alpha}]\,,

(4.3)

where
{\cal A}_{a}=A_{a}+B_{a}\gamma^{5}
and
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha+\beta\gamma^{5}
.
The corresponding field strength

\mathscr{F}_{ab}=\partial_{a}{\cal A}_{b}-\partial_{b}{\cal A}_{a}+[{\cal A}_{a},{\cal A}_{b}]=\hat{F}_{ab}+\hat{G}_{ab}\gamma^{5}

(4.4)

contains the Bardeen curvatures
\hat{F}_{ab}
and
\hat{G}_{ab}

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}A_{b}-\partial_{b}A_{a}+[A_{a},A_{b}]+[B_{a},B_{b}]\,,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}B_{b}-\partial_{b}B_{a}+[A_{a},B_{b}]+[B_{a},A_{b}]\,.

In the following we prefer to use the more explicit notation with
\gamma^{5}
.

We can use
A_{a}^{\alpha}
and
B_{a}^{\alpha}
as sources for the vector

J^{a\alpha}=i\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}T^{\alpha}\psi
and axial
J_{5}^{a\alpha}=i\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}\gamma^{5}T^{\alpha}\psi

currents, respectively.
These currents are on-shell covariantly conserved, with the conservation law reading

\displaystyle\left(D_{a}J^{a}\right)^{\alpha}=\partial_{a}J^{a\alpha}-i\overline{\psi}[A\!\!\!/+B\!\!\!\!/\,\gamma^{5},T^{\alpha}]\psi=0\,,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\left(D_{a}J^{a}_{5}\right)^{\alpha}=\partial_{a}J_{5}^{a\alpha}-i\overline{\psi}[A\!\!\!/\gamma^{5}+B\!\!\!\!/\,,T^{\alpha}]\psi=0\,,

or, equivalently, as

\displaystyle\left(D_{a}J^{a}\right)^{\alpha}=\partial_{a}J^{a\alpha}+f^{\alpha\beta\gamma}A_{a}^{\beta}J^{a\gamma}+f^{\alpha\beta\gamma}B_{a}^{\beta}J_{5}^{a\gamma}=0\,,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\left(D_{a}J^{a}_{5}\right)^{\alpha}=\partial_{a}J_{5}^{a\alpha}+f^{\alpha\beta\gamma}A_{a}^{\beta}J_{5}^{a\gamma}+f^{\alpha\beta\gamma}B_{a}^{\beta}J^{a\gamma}=0\,.

Indeed, under an infinitesimal gauge variation of the external sources
A_{a}^{\alpha}
and
B_{a}^{\alpha}
,
the action
S=\int d^{4}x\,{\cal L}
varies as

\delta^{(A,B)}S=-\int d^{4}x\,\left(\alpha^{\alpha}\left(D_{a}J^{a}\right)^{\alpha}+\beta^{\alpha}\left(D_{a}J_{5}^{a}\right)^{\alpha}\right)\;,

(4.8)

and the gauge symmetries imply that
J^{a\alpha}
and
J_{5}^{a\alpha}
are covariantly conserved on-shell, as stated above.

In order to study the stress tensor, we couple the theory to gravity by introducing
the vierbein
e_{\mu}{}^{a}
and related spin connection (see app.
B.2
).
Then the new lagrangian becomes

{\cal L}=-e\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}e_{a}{}^{\mu}\left(\partial_{\mu}+A_{\mu}+B_{\mu}\gamma^{5}+\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\gamma^{ab}\right)\psi=-e\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}e_{a}{}^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\psi\,,

(4.9)

where
e
is the determinant of the vierbein,
e_{a}{}^{\mu}
its inverse, and

\nabla_{\mu}
the covariant derivative that, since it acts on the fermion field, also contains the spin connection arising from the vierbein.
One may verify that, on top of the background general coordinates and local Lorentz symmetries,
the model acquires also a Weyl invariance, which through eq. (
B.26
) translates into an invariance under
arbitrary local scalings of the vierbein.
This suffices to prove conformal invariance in flat space, as we discussed in sec.
2.3
.
In fermionic theories, the vierbein is used also as an external source for the stress tensor, which we define as

T^{\mu a}=\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta e_{\mu a}}\;.

(4.10)

The Weyl symmetry implies that the stress tensor is traceless on-shell.
Indeed, an infinitesimal Weyl transformation with local parameter
\sigma
is of the form

\displaystyle\delta\psi

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\sigma\psi\,,

(4.11)

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}=\delta B_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta e_{\mu}{}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sigma e_{\mu}{}^{a}\,,

and varying the action under an infinitesimal Weyl transformation of the vierbein only produces the trace of the stress tensor

\delta^{(e)}S=\int d^{4}xe\,\sigma T^{a}{}_{a}\;.

(4.12)

Then, the full Weyl symmetry of the action implies that the trace vanishes on-shell,
T^{a}{}_{a}=0
.
On top of that, on-shell the stress tensor can be shown to be symmetric, once the curved index is made flat
with the vierbein (
T^{ab}=T^{ba}
), while satisfying a suitable conservation law.
For completeness, we report the stress tensor’s main properties relevant for our model and its explicit expression in app.
B.3
.

4.1.2
PV regularization

To regulate the one-loop effective action we introduce massive PV fields. The mass term produces the anomalies,
which we will compute with heat kernel methods following the scheme outlined in sec.
2.3.2
.

We denote by
\psi
the PV fields as well (as this should not cause any confusion)
and add a Dirac mass term

\Delta{\cal L}=-M\overline{\psi}\psi=\frac{M}{2}\left(\psi^{T}_{c}C\psi+\psi^{T}C\psi_{c}\right)

(4.13)

to the massless lagrangian of the PV field which is as in (
4.1
).
The Dirac mass preserves vector gauge invariance but breaks axial gauge invariance. Indeed under (
4.2
)
the mass term varies as

\delta\Delta{\cal L}=2M\overline{\psi}\beta\gamma^{5}\psi=-M\left(\psi^{T}_{c}\beta C\gamma^{5}\psi+\psi^{T}\beta^{T}C\gamma^{5}\psi_{c}\right)\,,

(4.14)

where we remind that
\beta=-i\beta^{\alpha}T^{\alpha}
, and it shows that the vector gauge symmetry is preserved, leaving room for an anomaly in the axial gauge symmetry.

The mass term sources also a trace anomaly, as the curved space version of (
4.13
)

\Delta{\cal L}=-eM\overline{\psi}\psi=\frac{eM}{2}\left(\psi^{T}_{c}C\psi+\psi^{T}C\psi_{c}\right)

(4.15)

varies under the infinitesimal Weyl transformation (
4.11
) as

\delta\Delta{\cal L}=-e\sigma M\overline{\psi}\psi=\frac{e\sigma M}{2}\left(\psi^{T}_{c}C\psi+\psi^{T}C\psi_{c}\right)\;.

(4.16)

However, the mass term preserves the general coordinate and local Lorentz symmetries.
We conclude that only axial gauge and trace anomalies are to be expected.

Casting the PV lagrangian
{\cal L}_{\scriptscriptstyle PV}={\cal L}+\Delta{\cal L}

in the form

{\cal L}_{\scriptscriptstyle PV}=\frac{1}{2}\phi^{T}T{\cal O}\phi+\frac{1}{2}M\phi^{T}T\phi\,,

(4.17)

where
\phi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi\\
\psi_{c}\end{array}\right)
, allows us to recognize the operators
1
1

1

We prefer to use
\psi_{c}=C^{-1}\overline{\psi}^{T}
rather than
\overline{\psi}
, as the former has the same index structure of

\psi
, and thus lives in the same spinor space.

T{\cal O}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&CD\!\!\!\!/\,\left(-A^{T},B^{T}\right)\\
CD\!\!\!\!/\,(A,B)&0\end{array}\right)\,,\qquad T=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&C\\
C&0\end{array}\right)\,,

(4.18)

and

{\cal O}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D\!\!\!\!/\,(A,B)&0\\
0&D\!\!\!\!/\,\left(-A^{T},B^{T}\right)\end{array}\right)\,,\qquad{\cal O}^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}(A,B)&0\\
0&D\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}\left(-A^{T},B^{T}\right)\end{array}\right)\,.

(4.19)

The latter identifies the regulators, as discussed in sec.
2.3.2
.
However, when deriving the formulae (
2.3.2
), (
2.85
), (
2.88
), (
2.90
) for a fermionic theory, an overall minus sign appears in the final results due to the Grassmannian nature of the field.
Thus, the formula that will be relevant in the present context for the anomaly calculations is

i\langle\delta S\rangle=-\lim_{M\to\infty}{\rm Tr}\left[Je^{-\frac{\mathcal{R}}{M^{2}}}\right]\,.

(4.20)

We recall that

J=K+\frac{1}{2}T^{-1}\delta T+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\delta{\cal O}}{M}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{R}}=-{\cal O}^{2}\,,

(4.21)

where
K
is read from the field transformation
\delta\phi=K\phi
which is a symmetry of the massless action.
Thus, in the case of the Bardeen model, for each of the symmetries we outlined we can construct the corresponding
J
and then derive the heat kernel traces that compute the anomalies.

To start with, the vector current
J^{a\alpha}
remains covariantly conserved also at the quantum level, as the PV mass term is invariant under vector gauge transformations.

For the axial current, recalling the transformation laws in (
4.2
), we find

J=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i\beta^{\alpha}T^{\alpha}\gamma^{5}&0\\
0&i\beta^{\alpha}T^{\alpha T}\gamma^{5}\end{array}\right)

(4.22)

as
\delta T
vanishes, while we discarded the contributions from
\delta{\cal O}
as they would vanish under the Dirac trace.
Here,
T^{\alpha T}
denotes the transposed of
T^{\alpha}
.
Considering now (
4.20
), (
4.8
) and (
2.102
) we find

(D_{a}\langle J^{a}_{5}\rangle)^{\alpha}=\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[\gamma^{5}T^{\alpha}a_{2}({R}_{\psi})\right]+{\rm tr}\left[\gamma^{5}T^{\alpha T}a_{2}({R}_{\psi_{c}})\right]\right\}\,,

(4.23)

where the remaining trace is the finite dimensional one on the gamma matrices and color space.
Here, we find the Seeley-DeWitt coefficients
a_{2}({R_{i}})
corresponding to the regulators
R_{i}

associated to the fields assembled into
\phi
that we read from (
4.21
) and (
4.19
), explicitly

{R}_{\psi}=-D\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}(A,B)\;,\qquad{R}_{\psi_{c}}=-D\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}\left(-A^{T},B^{T}\right)\;.

(4.24)

The
a_{2}
coefficients are the only ones that survive renormalization and the limit
{M\to\infty}
.

Similarly, for the Weyl symmetry and the transformations in (
4.11
) we find

\langle T^{a}{}_{a}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[a_{2}\left({R}_{\psi}\right)\right]+{\rm tr}\left[a_{2}\left({R}_{\psi_{c}}\right)\right]\right\}\,,

(4.25)

where now also
\delta T
contributes to (
4.21
), while
\delta{\cal O}
drops out as before.
Again, all remaining traces are in spinor and color spaces.

Finally, since the mass term is invariant under general coordinate and local Lorentz transformations, no anomalies arise in those symmetries.

4.1.3
Anomalies

We compute now the anomalies produced by the traces of the heat kernel coefficients
a_{2}
in
(
4.23
) and (
4.25
), with the regulators (
4.24
).
We limit ourselves to show the results here, but we report more details of the computations in app.
B.4
.

The vector symmetry is guaranteed to remain anomaly free by the invariance of the mass term. As a check
one may verify, using the explicit traces given in appendix
B.4
, that the would-be anomaly vanishes

\left(D_{a}\langle J^{a}\rangle\right)^{\alpha}=\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[T^{\alpha}a_{2}\left({R}_{\psi}\right)\right]-{\rm tr}\left[T^{\alpha T}a_{2}\left({R}_{\psi_{c}}\right)\right]\right\}=0\;.

(4.26)

Chiral anomaly

Performing the spinor traces in (
4.23
) results in the following expression for the chiral anomaly

\displaystyle\left(D_{a}\langle J^{a}_{5}\rangle\right)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}\epsilon^{abcd}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}T^{\alpha}\bigg[\hat{F}_{ab}\hat{F}_{cd}+\frac{1}{3}\hat{G}_{ab}\hat{G}_{cd}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{8}{3}\left(\hat{F}_{ab}B_{c}B_{d}+B_{a}\hat{F}_{bc}B_{d}+B_{a}B_{b}\hat{F}_{cd}\right)+\frac{32}{3}B_{a}B_{b}B_{c}B_{d}\bigg]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+PETs\,,

(4.27)

where the remaining trace is only in color space.

PETs
indicate the parity-even terms that take the form

\displaystyle PETs

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{2}}{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}T^{\alpha}\bigg[\frac{4}{3}D^{2}DB+\frac{2}{3}[\hat{F}^{ab},\hat{G}_{ab}]+\frac{8}{3}[D^{a}\hat{F}_{ab},B^{b}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{4}{3}\{B^{2},DB\}+8B_{a}DBB^{a}+\frac{8}{3}\{\{B^{a},B^{b}\},D_{a}B_{b}\}\bigg]\,,

(4.28)

where
DB=D_{a}B^{a}
,
D^{2}=D^{a}D_{a}
and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is the anti-commutator.
They are canceled by the chiral gauge variation of a local counterterm

\displaystyle\Gamma_{ct}=\int\frac{d^{4}x}{(4\pi)^{2}}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{2}{3}\left(D^{a}B^{b}\right)\left(D_{a}B_{b}\right)+4F^{ab}(A)B_{a}B_{b}-\frac{8}{3}B^{4}+\frac{4}{3}B^{a}B^{b}B_{a}B_{b}\bigg]

(4.29)

and the remaining expression coincides with the famous result obtained by Bardeen
[
Bar69aa
]
.

Trace anomaly

Evaluation of the spinor traces in (
4.25
) produces the trace anomaly

\displaystyle\langle T^{a}{}_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}\bigg[\frac{2}{3}\hat{F}^{ab}\hat{F}_{ab}+\frac{2}{3}\hat{G}^{ab}\hat{G}_{ab}\bigg]+CTTs\,,

(4.30)

where
CTTs
are the cohomologically trivial terms

CTTs=-\frac{4}{3}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}\left[D^{2}B^{2}+DBDB-\left(D^{a}B^{b}\right)\left(D_{b}B_{a}\right)-2F^{ab}(A)B_{a}B_{b}\right]\,.

(4.31)

They are canceled by the Weyl variation of the following counterterm

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}_{ct}=\int\frac{d^{4}x\sqrt{g}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{2}{3}\left(D^{\mu}B^{\nu}\right)\left(D_{\mu}B_{\nu}\right)+4F^{\mu\nu}(A)B_{\mu}B_{\nu}+\frac{1}{3}RB^{2}\bigg]\,,

(4.32)

where
\mu,\nu
are curved indices, and
R
the Ricci scalar. Of course, one restricts to flat space after variation.

The counterterms (
4.29
) and (
4.32
) merge
consistently into a unique counterterm that in curved space reads

\Gamma^{tot}_{ct}=\int\frac{d^{4}x\sqrt{g}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}\bigg[\frac{2}{3}(D^{\mu}B^{\nu})^{2}+4F^{\mu\nu}(A)B_{\mu}B_{\nu}+\frac{1}{3}RB^{2}-\frac{8}{3}B^{4}+\frac{4}{3}B^{\mu}B^{\nu}B_{\mu}B_{\nu}\bigg]\,.

(4.33)

We notice that, on top of the complete gauge invariance of the trace anomaly, there is no parity-odd term present.

4.1.4
Chiral and trace anomalies of Weyl fermions

We are now ready to study the chiral limit of the Bardeen model, and identify the chiral and trace anomalies
of Weyl fermions.
We take the limit
A_{a}=B_{a}\to A_{a}/2
, which creates a chiral projector in the coupling to the gauge field.
Then,
\hat{F}_{ab}=\hat{G}_{ab}\to F_{ab}(A)/2

and
J^{a}=J^{a}_{5}\to J_{a}=\frac{1}{2}(J^{a}+J^{a}_{5})
, so that from (
4.1.3
) and (
4.30
) we immediately derive the searched for anomalies for the
left-handed Weyl fermions coupled to non-abelian gauge fields

\displaystyle(D_{a}\langle J^{a}\rangle)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}\epsilon^{abcd}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}T^{\alpha}\partial_{a}\bigg[\frac{2}{3}A_{b}\partial_{c}A_{d}+\frac{1}{3}A_{b}A_{c}A_{d}\bigg]\,,

(4.34)

\displaystyle\langle T^{a}{}_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}{\rm tr}_{{}_{\!\!YM}}\bigg[\frac{1}{3}F^{ab}F_{ab}\bigg]\,,

(4.35)

where we discarded the cohomologically trivial terms.

The chiral anomaly is the standard one, rederived as a check on the methods used here.
The trace anomaly is our new result, that verifies
the absence of parity-odd terms. It is just half the trace anomaly of non-chiral Dirac fermions.

Thus, we have computed the consistent anomalies for Dirac and Weyl fermions,
defined as arising from the symmetry
variation of an effective action. In particular, we find that the consistent trace anomaly acquires
a gauge invariant form.
The property of gauge invariance of the trace anomaly is not explicitly maintained by
our regulator (as far as the axial gauge symmetry is concerned),
but the breaking terms can be removed by
the variation of a local counterterm, as we have indicated.

4.2
Axial gravity background

Following the approach by Bardeen to analyse the anomalies of chiral fermions by taking appropriate limits on the gauge background to which Dirac fermions are coupled to,
a metric-axial-tensor (MAT) background has been constructed in
[
Bon+17aa
,
Bon+18aa
]
.
In particular, the MAT background generalizes to curved space the approach used by Bardeen:
Dirac fermions are coupled to a metric-axial-tensor defined as

\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}+\gamma^{5}f_{\mu\nu}\,,

(4.36)

and suitable limits of the MAT background reproduce different theories of (chiral) fermions.
In the present section we will study the classical symmetries of this model and compute its anomalies.

4.2.1
The MAT model

The axial extension of the metric (
4.36
) induces similar (i.e with a
\gamma^{5}
component) extensions to the other geometrical quantities, like the vierbein
\hat{e}_{\mu}{}^{a}

and the spin connection
\hat{\omega}_{\mu ab}
.
Thus, a massless Dirac fermion coupled to the MAT background has a lagrangian of the form

\mathcal{L}=-\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}\sqrt{\hat{g}}\hat{e}_{a}{}^{\mu}\left(\partial_{\mu}+\frac{1}{4}\hat{\omega}_{\mu ab}\gamma^{ab}\right)\psi=-\overline{\psi}\gamma^{a}\sqrt{\hat{g}}\hat{e}_{a}{}^{\mu}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\psi\,,

(4.37)

with
\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}
the MAT covariant derivative.
All quantities with a hat contain an axial extension with
\gamma^{5}
and appear
always sandwiched between the Dirac spinors
\overline{\psi}
and
\psi
.
For a comprehensive presentation of the MAT model we refer to
[
Bon+17aa
,
Bon+18aa
]
.

For our purposes it is more convenient to split the Dirac fermion
\psi
into its two independent and Lorentz
irreducible chiral components
\lambda
and
\rho
of opposite chiralities,
\psi=\lambda+\rho
with
\gamma^{5}
-eigenvalues
\gamma^{5}\lambda=\lambda
and
\gamma^{5}\rho=-\rho
.
Then, the lagrangian takes the form

\mathcal{L}=-\sqrt{g_{+}}\,\overline{\lambda}\gamma^{a}e^{+\mu}_{a}\nabla^{+}_{\mu}\lambda-\sqrt{g_{-}}\,\overline{\rho}\gamma^{a}e^{-\mu}_{a}\nabla^{-}_{\mu}\rho\,,

(4.38)

where
g^{\pm}_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}\pm f_{\mu\nu}
are two different effective metrics, with
related compatible vierbeins, spin connections, and covariant derivatives
(which we indicate with the
\pm
sub/superscripts).
This happens since the
\gamma^{5}
matrices acting on chiral fermions are substituted by
the corresponding eigenvalues.
One could be more general, allowing also for the spacetime points to have an axial extension,
as outlined in
[
Bon+18aa
]
, but the present formulation is sufficient for our purposes.

The limit
f_{\mu\nu}=0
recovers the standard massless Dirac fermion in the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
.
Setting
g_{\mu\nu}=f_{\mu\nu}\to g_{\mu\nu}/2
produces instead a left handed chiral
fermion
\lambda
coupled to the final metric
g_{\mu\nu}
, while the other chirality is projected out since it would remain coupled to the metric
g^{-}_{\mu\nu}=0
.
A less singular limit, which keeps a free propagating right-handed fermion, consists in setting
g^{+}_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}
and
g^{-}_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}
,
with
\eta_{\mu\nu}
the flat Minkowski metric.

The Dirac theory in the MAT background has several symmetries which may become anomalous,
and limits on the background can be used to recover the anomalies of a chiral fermion, as in the Bardeen method.
The classical symmetries of the model are: diffeomorphisms,
local Lorentz transformation and Weyl rescalings,
together with their axial extensions, all of which are background symmetries since
they act on the MAT fields as well. In addition, the model admits
global vector and axial
U(1)
symmetries, that rotate the spinors by a phase.
This global
U(1)_{V}\times U(1)_{A}
symmetry group does
not act on the MAT background or any other background,
as for simplicity we do not couple the model to the corresponding
abelian gauge fields, although that could be done as well.
We will review shortly these symmetries, compute systematically
all of their anomalies, and then study the chiral limit.

Let us start by considering invariance under diffeomorphisms and their axial extension.
The change of coordinates
x^{\mu}\to x^{\mu}-\xi^{\mu}(x)
induce the standard transformation law on the fields as generated by the Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\xi}

\displaystyle\delta e^{\pm a}_{\mu}={\cal L}_{\xi}e^{\pm a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\xi^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}e^{\pm a}_{\mu}+(\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\nu})e^{\pm a}_{\nu}\,,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\delta\psi={\cal L}_{\xi}\psi

\displaystyle=\xi^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi\,,

which are a symmetry of the action defined with the lagrangian (
4.37
).
However, we can also define chiral transformation rules that leave the action invariant.
We may define left infinitesimal diffeomorphisms generated by a vector field
\xi_{+}^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle\delta e^{+a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle={\cal L}_{\xi_{+}}e^{+a}_{\mu}\,,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle={\cal L}_{\xi_{+}}\lambda\,,

\displaystyle\delta e^{-a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta\rho

\displaystyle=0\,,

and right infinitesimal diffeomorphisms generated by a vector field
\xi_{-}^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle\delta e^{+a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.41)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta e^{-a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle={\cal L}_{\xi_{-}}e^{-a}_{\mu}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\rho

\displaystyle={\cal L}_{\xi_{-}}\rho\,.

It is only the sum with local parameters identified, i.e. with
\xi^{\mu}=\xi_{+}^{\mu}=\xi_{-}^{\mu}
, that
plays the role of the geometrical transformation induced by the translation of the
spacetime point
x^{\mu}
described above.
Nevertheless, they are independent symmetries of the action.
They acquire a clear geometrical meaning once the spacetime point
x^{\mu}
is extended to have
an axial partner
[
Bon+18aa
]
, but we do not need to do that for the scope of the present
analysis. These symmetries
imply that the stress tensor and its axial partner satisfy suitable covariant conservation laws.
Indeed, by varying the action under the two effective vierbeins
e^{\pm}_{\mu a}
we can define two stress tensors as

T_{\pm}^{\mu a}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{\pm}}}\frac{\delta S}{\delta e^{\pm}_{\mu a}(x)}\,.

(4.42)

As we will comment in the next section, our regularization will not spoil these symmetries and no anomaly will arise in the conservation of the stress tensor at the quantum level.

The action is also invariant under the local Lorentz symmetries
that act independently on the
+
(left) and
-
(right) sector.
On the
+
sector, the left-handed local Lorentz symmetry acts by

\displaystyle\delta e^{+a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\omega^{+a}{}_{b}e^{+b}_{\mu}\,,

(4.43)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\omega^{+}_{ab}\gamma^{ab}\lambda\,,

\displaystyle\delta e^{-a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta\rho

\displaystyle=0\,,

where
\omega^{+}_{ab}=-\omega^{+}_{ba}
are local parameters.
Similarly, on the right sector we have

\displaystyle\delta e^{+a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.44)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta e^{-a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\omega^{-a}{}_{b}e^{-b}_{\mu}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\omega^{-}_{ab}\gamma^{ab}\rho\,.

These transformations leave the action invariant, so that the stress tensor and its axial companion are symmetric.

As for the Weyl and axial Weyl symmetries, it is again more convenient to consider their
\pm
linear combinations, that act separately on the chiral sectors of the theory.
The infinitesimal Weyl symmetries are defined by

\displaystyle\delta e_{\mu}^{\pm a}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\pm}e_{\mu}^{\pm a}\,,

(4.45)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\sigma^{+}\lambda\,,

\displaystyle\delta\rho

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\sigma^{-}\rho\,,

where
\sigma^{\pm}
are the two independent Weyl local parameters.
Invariance of the action under these transformations implies tracelessness of the stress tensor and its axial partner.

Finally, for convenience also in the case of the vector and axial
U(1)
symmetries we study their
\pm
linear combinations, which produce global
U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}
symmetries.
They have the following transformation rules

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=i\alpha^{+}\lambda\,,

(4.46)

\displaystyle\delta\rho

\displaystyle=i\alpha^{-}\rho\,,

where
\alpha^{\pm}
are independent infinitesimal parameters.
By Noether theorem we find the covariantly conserved Noether currents

\delta S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{g_{+}}\,\alpha_{+}\nabla^{+}_{\mu}J_{+}^{\mu}+\int d^{4}x\sqrt{g_{-}}\,\alpha_{-}\nabla^{-}_{\mu}J_{-}^{\mu}\,,

(4.47)

where the constants
\alpha_{\pm}
in (
4.46
) are extended to arbitrary functions,
with the currents taking the explicit form

J_{+}^{\mu}=i\overline{\lambda}\gamma^{a}e^{+\mu}_{a}\lambda
and

J_{-}^{\mu}=i\overline{\rho}\gamma^{a}e^{-\mu}_{a}\rho
.

4.2.2
PV regularization

We regulate the one-loop effective action of the Dirac theory in the MAT background with massive PV fields.
As for Bardeen model, the mass term produces the anomalies,
which we will compute with heat kernel methods following the scheme outlined in sec.
2.3.2
.

In this case we choose PV fields with a lagrangian of the same form as (
4.38
),
but augmented by a Majorana mass coupled to the MAT background as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle M}{\cal L}=\frac{M}{2}\sqrt{g_{+}}\,\left(\lambda^{T}C\lambda+h.c.\right)+\frac{M}{2}\sqrt{g_{-}}\,\left(\rho^{T}C\rho+h.c.\right)\,,

(4.48)

where
h.c.
denotes hermitian conjugation and
C
is the charge conjugation matrix.
For notational simplicity we use the same symbols for the PV fields and the original variables,
since no confusion should arise in the following.
The advantage of this specific mass term is that it is invariant under the left and right diffeomorphisms (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), and thus guarantees
the absence of gravitational anomalies
[
AW84aa
]
and the covariant conservation of the stress tensor at the quantum level.

Similarly, the mass term is invariant under the local Lorentz symmetries (
4.43
) and (
4.44
) that act independently on the
+
and
-
sector.
These are full symmetries of the total PV lagrangian, and the invariance of the regulating fields guarantees that the
stress tensor and its axial companion remain symmetric at the quantum level.

The only possible anomalies appear in the Weyl and axial Weyl symmetries, and in the vector and axial
U(1)
symmetries.
Under the Weyl transformations in (
4.45
), the mass term transforms as follows

\delta\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle M}{\cal L}=\sigma^{+}\frac{M}{2}\sqrt{g_{+}}\,(\lambda^{T}C\lambda+h.c.)+\sigma^{-}\frac{M}{2}\sqrt{g_{-}}\,(\rho^{T}C\rho+h.c.)\,,

(4.49)

so that it breaks the Weyl symmetries causing anomalies to appear.
The mass term also breaks the vector and axial
U(1)
symmetry of the massless action, since under (
4.46
) it transforms as

\delta\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle M}{\cal L}=i\alpha^{+}M\sqrt{g_{+}}\,(\lambda^{T}C\lambda-h.c.)+i\alpha^{-}M\sqrt{g_{-}}\,(\rho^{T}C\rho-h.c.)\,.

(4.50)

Before computing the anomalies, let us cast the lagrangian with the Majorana mass term
using the Dirac basis of spinors
\psi
and
\psi_{c}
,
2
2

2

See footnote
1
.
so to recognize the operators in
(
2.79
) and identify our regulator
{\cal O}^{2}
.
The massless lagrangian (
4.37
) with the addition of the Majorana mass term
(
4.48
) fixes the PV lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{PV}=\frac{1}{2}\psi_{c}^{T}C\sqrt{\bar{\hat{g}}}\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}\psi+\frac{1}{2}\psi^{T}C\sqrt{\hat{g}}\bar{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}\psi_{c}+\frac{M}{2}\left(\psi^{T}\sqrt{\hat{g}}C\psi+\psi_{c}^{T}\sqrt{\bar{\hat{g}}}C\psi_{c}\right)\,,

(4.51)

where a bar indicates a sign change in the axial extension
(e.g.
\bar{\hat{g}}_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}-\gamma^{5}f_{\mu\nu}
)
and
\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}=\gamma^{a}\hat{e}_{a}^{\mu}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}
,
so that on the field basis
\phi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi\\
\psi_{c}\end{array}\right)
we recognize

T\mathcal{O}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&C\sqrt{\hat{g}}\bar{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}\\
C\sqrt{\bar{\hat{g}}}{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}&0\end{array}\right)\;,\quad T=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{\hat{g}}C&0\\
0&\sqrt{\bar{\hat{g}}}C\end{array}\right)\;,\quad{\cal O}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\bar{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}\\
{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}&0\end{array}\right)\,,

(4.52)

and the regulator

{\cal O}^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\bar{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}&0\\
0&{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}\bar{\hat{\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,}}\end{array}\right)\,,\quad\quad\mathcal{R}=-\mathcal{O}^{2}\,,

(4.53)

which appear in the heat kernel formulae as in (
4.20
).
The regulator structure is perhaps more transparent
when the Dirac fermions are split in their chiral parts

\phi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda\\
\rho\\
\rho_{c}\\
\lambda_{c}\end{array}\right)\,,

(4.54)

and one recognizes a block diagonal operator

\mathcal{R}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}R_{\lambda}&0&0&0\\
0&R_{\rho}&0&0\\
0&0&R_{\rho_{c}}&0\\
0&0&0&R_{\lambda_{c}}\end{array}\right)\,,

(4.55)

with entries

\displaystyle R_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{+}P_{+}\,,\qquad R_{\lambda_{c}}=-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{+}P_{-}\,,

(4.56)

\displaystyle R_{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{-}P_{-}\,,\qquad R_{\rho_{c}}=-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{-}P_{+}\,.

where we have used the left and right chiral projectors

P_{+}=P_{L}={(\mathbb{I}+\gamma^{5})}/{2}
and
P_{-}=P_{R}={(\mathbb{I}-\gamma^{5})}/{2}
,
and denoted by
\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,_{\pm}=\gamma^{a}e^{\pm\mu}_{a}\nabla^{\pm}_{\mu}

the Dirac operators coupled to the
\pm
effective vierbeins.

Before computing the anomalies, let us stress that the choice of the mass term to be used in the PV sector is quite arbitrary, and it is most convenient to choose the one which preserves the maximal number of symmetries at the quantum level.
The choice is essentially between the Dirac and Majorana masses, suitably coupled to the MAT background, but the Majorana mass is the one which breaks the least number of symmetries.
This happens because the Majorana mass keeps a split structure for the couplings of the chiral irreducible components of the Dirac fermion
to the effective metrics
g^{\pm}_{\mu\nu}
, and produces
anomalies in the Weyl and
U(1)
symmetries and their axial extensions only.
We provide more details on the regularisation with a Dirac mass in app.
B.5
.
This mass term turns out to be much less symmetric since it destroys the axial symmetries.
It could be employed as well, but calculations become much more cumbersome, producing more anomalies
then necessary, that eventually must be cured by adding countertems to the effective action.
However, let us recall once more that any choice of the PV mass term is valid, as local counterterms can
be added to the effective action to recover the same final result, independently
of the regularization scheme adopted.
This arbitrariness is a general feature of the renormalization process of QFTs.

4.2.3
Anomalies

Let us now compute the anomalies. For the Weyl symmetries we get anomalies
in the traces of the stress tensors, as defined by
varying the action under the two effective vierbeins
e^{\pm}_{\mu a}
in (
4.42
).
In each chiral sector we use the corresponding chiral metric, and related vierbein, to perform covariant operations and take traces, and the calculation is just a double copy of the one
presented in
[
BM16aa
]
.
Recalling (
4.20
) and (
4.21
), we identify the structure of the breaking term
J
, entirely due
to the PV mass. The functional trace is then computed by using
the heat kernel coefficients
a_{2}(R_{i})
for the regulators
R_{i}
induced by the PV fields.
For the traces of the stress tensors on the MAT background we find

\displaystyle\langle T_{+}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[P_{+}a_{2}\left(R_{\lambda}\right)\right]+{\rm tr}\left[P_{-}a_{2}\left(R_{\lambda_{c}}\right)\right]\right\}\,,

(4.57)

\displaystyle\langle T_{-}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[P_{-}a_{2}\left(R_{\rho}\right)\right]+{\rm tr}\left[P_{+}a_{2}\left(R_{\rho_{c}}\right)\right]\right\}\,,

where the remaining final dimensional traces are traces on the gamma matrices.
The projectors in (
4.55
) can be dropped, as they get absorbed by the explicit projectors
already present in (
4.57
).
Thus, we may use
R_{\lambda}=R_{\lambda_{c}}=-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{+}
and
R_{\rho}=R_{\rho_{c}}=-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{-}
to simplify the anomaly expressions to

\langle T_{\pm}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2(4\pi)^{2}}{\rm tr}\left[a_{2}\left(-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{\pm}\right)\right]\,,

(4.58)

and we find the following trace anomalies on the MAT background

\langle T_{\pm}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle=\frac{1}{720(4\pi)^{2}}\left(7R_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}R^{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}+8R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}-5R^{2}+12\square R\right)\left(g_{\pm}\right)\,,

(4.59)

where the functional dependence on
g_{\pm}
reminds that all the geometrical quantities
and covariant operations are computed using the effective metric
g^{\pm}_{\mu\nu}
.

We now compute the anomalies in the conservation of the
U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}
currents determined in (
4.47
).
From the PV regularization we find the following expressions

\displaystyle\nabla^{+}_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{+}\rangle=\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[P_{+}a_{2}\left(R_{\lambda}\right)\right]-{\rm tr}\left[P_{-}a_{2}\left(R_{\lambda_{c}}\right)\right]\right\}\,,

(4.60)

\displaystyle\nabla^{-}_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{-}\rangle=\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left\{{\rm tr}\left[P_{-}a_{2}\left(R_{\rho}\right)\right]-{\rm tr}\left[P_{+}a_{2}\left(R_{\rho_{c}}\right)\right]\right\}\,,

that once more can be simplified to

\nabla^{\pm}_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{\pm}\rangle=\pm\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{2}}{\rm tr}\left[\gamma^{5}\,a_{2}\left(-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2}_{\pm}\right)\right]\;.

(4.61)

Their evaluation in terms of the heat kernel coefficients produces anomalies proportional
to the Pontryagin density of the effective metrics

\nabla^{\pm}_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{\pm}\rangle=\mp\frac{1}{48(4\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{g_{\pm}}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}(g_{\pm})\;.

(4.62)

The expressions (
4.59
) and (
4.62
) are our final results for the anomalies of a Dirac fermion on a MAT background. All other symmetries are anomaly free.
We have evaluated these anomalies using traces with chiral projectors of the heat kernel
coefficient
a_{2}(-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2})
, associated to the covariant square of the Dirac operator
in a background metric
g_{\mu\nu}
. For completeness, we list this coefficient and related traces

\displaystyle a_{2}(-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2})=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{180}\left(R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}-R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}\right)+\frac{1}{288}R^{2}-\frac{1}{120}\square R+\frac{1}{192}{\cal R}_{\mu\nu}{\cal R}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(4.63)

\displaystyle{\rm tr}[P_{\pm}a_{2}(-\nabla\!\!\!\!/\,^{2})]=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{720}\left(7R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+8R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}-5R^{2}+12\square R\right)

(4.64)

\displaystyle\pm\frac{i}{96}\sqrt{g}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}\,,

(4.65)

where
{\cal R}_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu ab}\gamma^{ab}
.
The coefficient
a_{2}
is deduced from (
2.101
) in the same way outlined in app.
B.4
(see also
[
DeW64aa
,
Vas03aa
]
);
these expressions have appeared in the anomaly context already in
[
CD78aa
,
CD79aa
]
.

4.2.4
Limits of the MAT background

We now discuss the limits on the MAT background to recover the theories of Dirac and Weyl
fermions in a curved spacetime and their anomalies.

Setting
f_{\mu\nu}=0
reproduces the standard coupling of a massless Dirac fermion to a curved
background and
corresponds to identifying the two effective metrics
g^{+}_{\mu\nu}=g^{-}_{\mu\nu}
.
The final stress tensor becomes the sum of the two chiral stress tensors, and acquires
the sum of the two trace anomalies in (
4.59
).
Thus, we recover the usual trace anomaly of a Dirac field
[
BM16aa
]

\displaystyle\langle T^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{360(4\pi)^{2}}\left(7R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+8R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}-5R^{2}+12\square R\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{180(4\pi)^{2}}\left(-\frac{11}{2}E_{4}+9C^{2}+6\square R\right)\,.

(4.66)

Similarly, for the two
U(1)
symmetries, we obtain

\nabla_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{\pm}\rangle=\mp\frac{1}{48(4\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{g}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}\,,

(4.67)

which gets translated into the covariant conservation of the vector current

J^{\mu}_{{}_{V}}=J^{\mu}_{+}+J^{\mu}_{-}
, together with
the anomalous conservation of the axial current
J^{\mu}_{{}_{A}}=J^{\mu}_{+}-J^{\mu}_{-}
,
with the well-known Pontryagin contribution
[
Kim69aa
,
DS72aa
]

\nabla_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{{}_{V}}\rangle=0\,,\qquad\nabla_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{{}_{A}}\rangle=-\frac{1}{24(4\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{g}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}\,.

(4.68)

Let us now study the case of the Weyl fermion
\lambda
.
This is obtained by taking the collapsing limit in which the effective metric
g^{-}_{\mu\nu}
becomes flat
(
g^{-}_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}
and
g^{+}_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}
), so that
the independent right-handed fermion
\rho
decouples completely from the background.
Therefore, only the chiral left-handed part contributes to the stress tensor, producing
for the trace anomaly half of the result above.
Similarly, we find the anomalous conservation of the
U(1)
current
J^{\mu}_{+}
,
the only one that remains coupled to the curved background,
with the expected Pontryagin contribution.
To summarize, we find for a left-handed Weyl fermion the following anomalies

\displaystyle\langle T^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{720(4\pi)^{2}}\left(7R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+8R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}-5R^{2}+12\square R\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{180(4\pi)^{2}}\left(-\frac{11}{4}E_{4}+\frac{9}{2}C^{2}+3\square R\right)\,,

(4.69)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{+}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{48(4\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{g}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}\,.

(4.70)

These results confirm the absence of a Pontryagin term in the trace anomaly of a Weyl fermion.
The Pontryagin term sits only in the chiral anomaly.

Kapitel
5
Irrelevant operators and their holographic anomalies

In sec.
2.4
we introduced the AdS/CFT correspondence as an example of a holographic duality, and we showed that the holographic computation of the CFT trace anomaly provides a test of the correspondence.
In particular, we introduced a subgroup of bulk diffeomorphisms, called PBH diffeomorphisms, that at the boundary reduce to a Weyl transformation of the boundary metric.
Then, evaluating a bulk action on the solution of the PBH equations yielded the trace anomaly of the CFT.

As we discussed in sec.
2.4.3
, bulk scalar fields are dual to scalar operators in the CFT.
Thus, the addition of scalar fields in the bulk allows to study the contributions of scalar operators to the trace anomaly of the CFT.
Indeed, this has been done shortly after the first holographic computation of the Weyl anomaly (see e.g.
[
HSS01aa
]
) for scalar fields dual to relevant or marginal operators.
Scalars dual to irrelevant operators are considered in
[
Ree11aab
]
and their contribution to the conformal anomaly is computed in
[
Ree11aac
]
in the case of a flat background metric at the boundary.
Nonetheless, when formulated on a curved background, CFTs with irrelevant operators of integer conformal dimension manifest peculiar properties
[
ST19aa
]
, namely the Weyl transformation of the metric acquires a beta-function which in turn deforms the trace anomaly.

In this chapter, we will provide a holographic description of the four-dimensional CFT studied in
[
ST19aa
]
.
To do so, in sec.
5.1
we modify the PBH transformations to include a massive scalar field in the bulk, that sources an integer-dimensional irrelevant operator on the boundary.
As we will show, the scalar field modifies the usual Weyl transformation of the boundary metric and we derive the modified Weyl anomaly of the four-dimensional boundary CFT in sec.
5.2
.
More details and explicit results are reported in app.
C
.
These results provide an additional test of the AdS/CFT correspondence and have been first published in
[
Bro22aa
]
, on which we base the following presentation.

5.1
Massive scalar field and modified PBH transformations

We start by adding a massive scalar field
\Phi
in the bulk and couple it to the metric, and extend the PBH transformations discussed in sec.
2.4.4
to describe this system.
The PBH transformation of scalar fields is also discussed in
[
BFS01aa
,
BFS02aa
,
Erd01aa
]
.

From the standard holographic dictionary (see sec.
2.4.3
) we known that a bulk scalar field of mass
m
is dual to a scalar operator on the boundary theory with dimension
\Delta
, related to the mass by
m^{2}=\Delta(\Delta-d)
.
1
1

1

We adopt here and in the following the same conventions employed in sec.
2.4.4
.

Close to the boundary, we consider the following expansion

\Phi(z,x)=z^{(d-\Delta)/2}\phi(z,x)\,,\quad\phi(z,x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\phi_{(n)}(x)z^{n}\,,

(5.1)

with
\phi_{(0)}
being the source of the boundary operator.
2
2

2

As for the metric expansion (
2.137
), there are logarithmic terms in the expansion of
\phi(z,x)
for even integer dimension
d
.
We assume we do not need to include them in the present discussion.

Requiring that the bulk scalar is indeed a scalar under diffeomorphisms,

\Phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})=\Phi(z,x)\,,

(5.2)

and choosing the diffeomorphism to be a PBH (
2.132
), we obtain the PBH transformation for the field
\phi
as

\delta\phi=-2\sigma\bigg(\frac{d-\Delta}{2}+z\partial_{z}\bigg)\phi+a^{i}\partial_{i}\phi\,.

(5.3)

With the expansion in (
5.1
), we get

\delta\phi_{(n)}=-\sigma(d+2n-\Delta)+\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\left(a^{i}_{(n-m)}\partial_{i}\phi_{(m)}\right)\,,

(5.4)

that to lowest order yields

\delta\phi_{(0)}=\sigma\left(\Delta-d\right)\phi_{(0)}\,,

(5.5)

namely the correct transformation of the source of a dimension
\Delta
operator under Weyl transformation.
Eventually, we want to make contact with the four-dimensional CFT analysed in
[
ST19aa
]
.
Thus, we choose
\Delta=d+1
, and therefore the scalar field is sourcing an irrelevant operator in the CFT.

When we couple the bulk scalar to gravity, the dynamical background will backreact and the metric in FG form will be as follows

ds^{2}=\frac{dz^{2}}{4z^{2}}+\frac{1}{z}\left(g_{ij}(z,x)+h_{ij}(z,x)\right)dx^{i}dx^{j}\,,

(5.6)

where
h_{ij}
is the backreaction, which depends explicitly on
g_{ij}(z,x)
and
\Phi(z,x)
.
We are essentially allowing for perturbations of the metric
g_{ij}
due to the presence of the scalar field
\Phi
, and the metric
g_{ij}
is then treated as a background, unperturbed, metric.
To first non-trivial order, the backreaction is quadratic in the scalar field, and from now on we will work to order
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,\phi^{2})
.
We impose a boundary condition for the backreaction (following
[
Ree11aab
]
), namely that the backreaction does not change the boundary metric.
In other words,
g^{(0)}_{ij}
is still the boundary metric even in the presence of the backreaction.
We will see the effect of this boundary condition later.

We now derive the modifications of the PBH transformations due to the presence of the backreaction by studying the behaviour of (
5.6
) under diffeomorphisms.
The FG form of the bulk metric in (
5.6
) is invariant under a general coordinate transformation
X^{\mu}=X^{\prime\mu}+\xi^{\mu}(X^{\prime})
for

\xi^{z}=-2z\sigma(x)\,,\quad\xi^{i}=a^{i}(z,x)+b^{i}(z,x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{j}\sigma(x)\int_{\epsilon}^{z}dz^{\prime}\left(g^{ij}(z^{\prime},x)-h^{ij}(z^{\prime},x)\right)\,,

(5.7)

where
b^{i}
contains the scalar field corrections brought about by the backreaction and is therefore of order
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,\phi^{2})
, while
a^{i}
is still of order
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,\phi^{0})
.
Notice that, contrary to eq. (
2.132
), now we are restricting the radial integration to the region
z\geq\epsilon>0
.
This is necessary to avoid divergences in the integration.
Indeed, since
\Delta=d+1
and
h_{ij}
is quadratic in the scalar field, it follows that the backreaction goes as
1/z
about the boundary, thus making the above integration divergent at
z=0
and requiring that we integrate over the region
z\geq\epsilon>0
.
This effect is reminiscent of
[
MMV18aa
,
Har+19aa
]
: the scalar field is causing the boundary to move into the bulk.
Finally, from form invariance of
G_{ij}
we find to
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,\phi^{2})

\displaystyle\delta g_{ij}+\delta h_{ij}

\displaystyle=2\sigma(1-z\partial_{z})\left(g_{ij}+h_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\nabla_{i}a_{j}+\nabla_{j}a_{i}+\nabla_{i}b_{j}+\nabla_{j}b_{i}+h_{ik}\nabla_{j}a^{k}+h_{jk}\nabla_{i}a^{k}+a^{k}\nabla_{k}h_{ij}\,,

(5.8)

where indices are lowered with (and derivatives are covariant w.r.t.)
g_{ij}
.
We refer to eqs. (
5.7
), (
5.1
) as the modified PBH transformations.

Given the leading asymptotic behaviour of the metric and the backreaction, we make the following Ansätze for the radial expansion of
a^{i}
and
b^{i}

\displaystyle a^{i}(z,x)

\displaystyle=\overline{a}^{i}(\epsilon,x)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}\,a^{i}_{(n)}(x)\,,

(5.9)

\displaystyle b^{i}(z,x)

\displaystyle=\overline{b}^{i}(\epsilon,x)+\log z\,\tilde{b}^{i}_{(1)}(x)+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\left[z^{n-1}\left(\log z\,\tilde{b}^{i}_{(n)}(x)+b^{i}_{(n)}(x)\right)\right]\,,

(5.10)

where
\overline{a}^{i}
and
\overline{b}^{i}
are constant terms in
z
and their appearance is due to the lower end of the integration in (
5.7
).
For the metric and backreaction we assume

\displaystyle g_{ij}(z,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z^{n}\,g^{(n)}_{ij}(x)\,,

(5.11)

\displaystyle h_{ij}(z,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z}h^{(0)}_{ij}(x)+\log z\,\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{ij}(x)+0+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\left[z^{n-1}\left(\log z\,\tilde{h}^{(n)}_{ij}(x)+h^{(n)}_{ij}(x)\right)\right]\,,

(5.12)

where we stress that there is no term at order
z^{0}
.
This implements the boundary condition that we anticipated above, namely that the boundary metric is still given by
g^{(0)}_{ij}
even in the presence of the backreaction
[
Ree11aab
]
.
The appearance of logarithmic terms is a consequence of the particular choice for the dimension of the scalar field
\Delta=d+1
, and we are thus generalising the analysis of
[
Ree11aab
]
as advocated in
[
Ree11aac
]
.

Using the above expansions, from the modified PBH equations we find for the first few terms (the
a^{i}_{(n)}
are as before)

\displaystyle\tilde{b}^{i}_{(1)}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{(0)}^{im}g_{(0)}^{jn}h^{(0)}_{mn}\partial_{j}\sigma\,,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\tilde{b}^{i}_{(2)}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{(0)}^{im}g_{(0)}^{jn}\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{mn}\partial_{j}\sigma\,,

(5.14)

\displaystyle b^{i}_{(2)}=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{2}\big[\big(g_{(0)}^{im}g_{(1)}^{jn}+g_{(1)}^{im}g_{(0)}^{jn}\big)h^{(0)}_{mn}+g_{(0)}^{im}g_{(0)}^{jn}\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{mn}\big]\partial_{j}\sigma\,,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\tilde{b}^{i}_{(3)}=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{4}\big[\big(g_{(0)}^{im}g_{(1)}^{jn}+g_{(1)}^{im}g_{(0)}^{jn}\big)\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{mn}-g_{(0)}^{im}g_{(0)}^{jn}\tilde{h}^{(2)}_{mn}\big]\partial_{j}\sigma\,,

(5.16)

\displaystyle b^{i}_{(3)}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\bigg[h_{(2)}^{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{h}_{(2)}^{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\big(g_{(1)}^{ik}\tilde{h}^{(1)j}{}_{k}+g_{(1)}^{jk}\tilde{h}^{(1)i}{}_{k}\big)+g_{(1)}^{ik}g_{(1)}^{jl}h^{(0)}_{kl}+g_{(1)}^{ik}g^{(1)l}{}_{k}h^{(0)j}{}_{l}

\displaystyle+g_{(1)}^{jk}g^{(1)l}{}_{k}h^{(0)i}{}_{l}-g_{(2)}^{ik}h^{(0)j}{}_{k}-g_{(2)}^{jk}h^{(0)i}{}_{k}\bigg]\partial_{j}\sigma\,,

(5.17)

where indices are lowered (raised) with the (inverse of)
g^{(0)}_{ij}
; curvatures and covariant derivatives will be w.r.t.
g^{(0)}_{ij}
.
For the metric and backreaction variation
3
3

3

The expressions that follow are written up to boundary diffeomorphisms generated by
\bar{a}^{i}
and
\bar{b}^{i}
. Since their presence does not affect the solution of the PBH equations, we discard them for simplicity of notation.

\displaystyle\delta h^{(0)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\;4\sigma h^{(0)}_{ij}\,,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\;2\sigma\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{ij}+\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}\tilde{b}_{(1)j}+\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\tilde{b}_{(1)i}\,,

(5.19)

\displaystyle\delta g^{(0)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\;2\sigma g^{(0)}_{ij}-2\sigma\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{ij}+h^{(0)}_{ik}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}a^{k}_{(1)}+h^{(0)}_{jk}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}a^{k}_{(1)}+a^{k}_{(1)}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{k}h^{(0)}_{ij}\,,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{h}^{(n+1)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\;2\sigma(1-n)\tilde{h}^{(n+1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=1}^{n+1}\Big(g^{(n-m+1)}_{ik}\partial_{j}\tilde{b}^{k}_{(m)}+g^{(n-m+1)}_{jk}\partial_{i}\tilde{b}^{k}_{(m)}+\tilde{b}^{k}_{(m)}\partial_{k}g^{(n-m+1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle+\tilde{h}^{(n-m+1)}_{ik}\partial_{j}a^{k}_{(m)}+\tilde{h}^{(n-m+1)}_{jk}\partial_{i}a^{k}_{(m)}+a^{k}_{(m)}\partial_{k}\tilde{h}^{(n-m+1)}_{ij}\Big)\,,

(5.21)

\displaystyle\delta g^{(n)}_{ij}+\delta h^{(n+1)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\,2\sigma(1-n)\left(g^{(n)}_{ij}+h^{(n+1)}_{ij}\right)-2\sigma\tilde{h}^{(n+1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=1}^{n+1}\Big(g^{(n-m+1)}_{ik}\partial_{j}{b}^{k}_{(m)}+g^{(n-m+1)}_{jk}\partial_{i}{b}^{k}_{(m)}+{b}^{k}_{(m)}\partial_{k}g^{(n-m+1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle+{h}^{(n-m+1)}_{ik}\partial_{j}a^{k}_{(m)}+{h}^{(n-m+1)}_{jk}\partial_{i}a^{k}_{(m)}+a^{k}_{(m)}\partial_{k}{h}^{(n-m+1)}_{ij}\Big)\,.

(5.22)

A few comments are in order here.
The above equation for the metric and the backreaction can be solved in the same spirit outlined in sec.
2.4.4
.
The term
h^{(n)}_{ij}
(and similarly
\tilde{h}^{(n)}_{ij}
) is a symmetric and covariant tensor built from
g^{(0)}_{ij}
and quadratic in
\phi_{(0)}
with
2n
derivatives.
Once the most general expression for
g^{(n)}_{ij}+h^{(n+1)}_{ij}
(or
\tilde{h}^{(n)}_{ij}
) is written down, it is enough to vary it according to (
5.20
) and (
5.5
) up to
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,\phi^{2})
and impose the variation is a PBH to find the sought for expression.
The solution of the backreacted Einstein’s equations of motion (as in
[
Ree11aab
]
but with
\Delta=d+1
) will also satisfy the above equations.

As in the pure gravity case, the modified PBH equations fix the expression of the backreaction only to some extent.
For instance, the first term in the expansion is

h^{(0)}_{ij}=h_{0}\,g^{(0)}_{ij}\phi_{(0)}^{2}

(5.23)

for some coefficient
h_{0}
, not fixed by the PBH equation (
5.18
).
The higher order terms in the radial expansion will have more and more free coefficients, that are fixed on-shell given an action.
4
4

4

For instance, given the action of a free massive scalar field coupled to a dynamical metric, then on-shell
h_{0}
is proportional to the coefficient of the lowest order term in the radial expansion of the scalar field action and it is thus non-vanishing on-shell.

Notice that the backreaction modifies the usual Weyl transformation of the boundary metric in (
5.20
).
However, unlike in the pure gravity case, as it stands the modified PBH transformation does not reduce to a Weyl transformation of
g^{(0)}_{ij}
.
Indeed, when the diffeomorphism is a modified PBH transformation, from (
2.135
) and (
5.7
) we find that (up to
\mathcal{O}(\sigma,\phi^{2})
)

\hat{\xi}^{z}=0\,,\quad\hat{\xi}^{i}=\epsilon\big(g^{ij}(\epsilon,x)-h^{ij}(\epsilon,x)\big)\big(\xi^{z}_{2}\partial_{j}\xi^{z}_{1}-\xi^{z}_{1}\partial_{j}\xi^{z}_{2}\big)\,,

(5.24)

so that now we are left with a residual diffeomorphism

[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]g^{(0)}_{ij}=g^{(0)}_{jk}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}\hat{\xi}^{k}+g^{(0)}_{ik}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\hat{\xi}^{k}\,.

(5.25)

Before solving the modified PBH equation for the backreaction, we thus have to address the issue of the
z=\epsilon
cutoff.

The holographic dual of the gravitational theory discussed so far is a CFT deformed by an irrelevant operator.
However, since we want to make contact with the unperturbed CFT presented in
[
ST19aa
]
, we have to move the cutoff surface back to the AdS boundary.
Given that

\bar{b}^{i}(\epsilon,x)=\frac{1}{2}h_{0}\log\epsilon\,g_{(0)}^{ij}\phi_{(0)}^{2}\partial_{j}\sigma+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(5.26)

the way to move the cutoff back to the boundary, without setting the source to zero, is to take
h_{0}=0
and then
\epsilon=0
.
In this limit we can describe a boundary CFT in the presence of an irrelevant operator, avoiding the prescription
\phi_{(0)}^{2}=0
advocated in
[
Wit98aa
,
HSS01aa
]
.
As a bonus, the commutator in (
5.25
) vanishes and the modified PBH transformation reduces to a Weyl transformation at the boundary.
However, the price to pay is that the solutions of the modified PBH equations are not on-shell anymore (see footnote
4
).

Now, we proceed by solving the modified PBH equations for the backreaction with
h_{0}=0
.
For
\tilde{h}_{(1)}
we make the Ansatz

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{(1)}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\;h_{1}\,\overset{(0)}{R}_{ij}\phi_{(0)}^{2}+h_{2}\,g^{(0)}_{ij}\overset{(0)}{R}\phi_{(0)}^{2}+h_{3}\,\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}\phi_{(0)}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\phi_{(0)}+h_{4}\,\phi_{(0)}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\phi_{(0)}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+h_{5}\,g^{(0)}_{ij}\phi_{(0)}\overset{(0)}{\Box}\phi_{(0)}+h_{6}\,g^{(0)}_{ij}(\overset{(0)}{\nabla}\phi_{(0)})^{2}\,,

(5.27)

and a solution of (
5.19
) is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{(1)ij}=

\displaystyle\;\,h_{1}\left(\overset{(0)}{R}_{ij}\phi_{(0)}^{2}+(d-2)\phi_{(0)}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{i}\overset{(0)}{\nabla}_{j}\phi_{(0)}+g^{(0)}_{ij}\phi_{(0)}\overset{(0)}{\Box}\phi_{(0)}-(d-1)g^{(0)}_{ij}(\overset{(0)}{\nabla}\phi_{(0)})^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\,h_{2}\,g^{(0)}_{ij}\left(\overset{(0)}{R}\phi_{(0)}^{2}+2(d-1)\phi_{(0)}\overset{(0)}{\Box}\phi_{(0)}-d(d-1)(\overset{(0)}{\nabla}\phi_{(0)})^{2}\right)\,.

(5.28)

Notice that the solutions parametrised by
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are proportional to
\hat{R}_{ij}
and
\hat{R}
respectively, which are the curvature tensors computed from the Weyl invariant metric
g^{(0)}_{ij}/\phi^{2}_{(0)}
.
Then, the variation of the metric in (
5.20
) reads

\delta g^{(0)}_{ij}=2\sigma g^{(0)}_{ij}-2\sigma\beta_{ij}\,,\quad\text{with}\quad\beta_{ij}=h_{1}\,\hat{R}_{ij}+h_{2}\,g^{(0)}_{ij}\hat{R}\,,

(5.29)

and we interpret it as a modification of the usual Weyl transformation of the boundary metric due to the presence of the scalar field.
The holographic beta-function that we find
is in agreement with
[
ST19aa
]
.
Notice that for
h_{1}=0
we could perform a field dependent redefinition of
\sigma
, so that the metric transformation is still a usual Weyl transformation:

\sigma\to\hat{\sigma}=\sigma\left(1-h_{2}\,\hat{R}\right)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\delta g^{(0)}_{ij}=2\hat{\sigma}g^{(0)}_{ij}\,.

(5.30)

We will comment on the effect of this redefinition to the holographic anomalies later.

Similarly, we also solve (
5.21
) for
\tilde{h}_{(2)}
and (
5.22
) for
g_{(1)}+h_{(2)}
.
The Ansatz for
h_{(2)}
has thirty-five terms and the modified PBH equation leaves six out of the thirty-five coefficients free, while
\tilde{h}_{(2)}
is determined in terms of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
.
We also solve for the trace of
\tilde{h}_{(3)}
and the trace of
h_{(3)}
that we will need in the following.
The Ansatz for the trace of
h_{(3)}
has sixty-six terms and the modified PBH equation leaves nine coefficients free, while the trace of
\tilde{h}_{(3)}
is determined in terms of
h_{1},h_{2}
and the
c_{1},c_{2}
appearing in (
2.144
).
The expressions of
g_{(1)}
and
g_{(2)}
are not modified by the presence of the backreaction and are still given by (
2.143
) and (
2.144
) respectively.
We provide more details in app.
C.1
.

5.2
Effective boundary action and modified Weyl anomalies

To derive the holographic dual of the modified Weyl anomaly found in
[
ST19aa
]
, we extend the method outlined in sec.
2.4.5
to include the effect of the backreaction.

Consider an action

S=\int_{M}d^{d+1}X\sqrt{G}f\left(R(G),\Phi\right)\,,

(5.31)

where
f
is a local function of the curvature and its covariant derivatives and contains also matter field
\Phi
.
We could think of
S
as gravitational action with a scalar field coupled to the metric and we require that
f\left(R(G),\Phi=0\right)
is such that
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
is a solution of the equations of motion.
Then, we write the action in FG coordinates with backreaction (
5.6
) and, expanding in powers of
z
, we obtain the holographic anomalies by evaluating the corresponding expressions on the solutions of the modified PBH equations.
However, the off-shell solution that we discussed so far with
h_{0}=0
sets the scalar field action to zero (see footnote
4
), so that eventually the scalar field contributions to the anomalies is only due to the backreaction.

Defining now
\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{G}f\left(R(G),\Phi\right)
, in the FG coordinates (
5.6
) we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{g_{(0)}}z^{-d/2-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{g}+\mathcal{L}_{h}\right)\,,

(5.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}^{(n)}_{g}(x)z^{n}\,,\quad\mathcal{L}_{h}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\log z\,\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{h}^{(n)}+\mathcal{L}_{h}^{(n)}\right]\,,

(5.33)

where
\mathcal{L}_{h}
contains the backreaction and it is thus quadratic in the scalar field; in the second line we use the expansions (
5.12
) with
h_{0}=0
and
\mathcal{L}_{g}
is as in (
2.147
).
Following the reasoning of sec.
2.4.5
, we can show that
[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]\mathcal{L}=0
upon using the PBH group property for
h_{0}=0
and thus
\mathcal{L}_{g}+\mathcal{L}_{h}
satisfies the following WZ condition

\delta_{1}\int_{\partial M}d^{d}x\sqrt{g_{(0)}}\,\sigma_{2}(x)(\mathcal{L}_{g}+\mathcal{L}_{h})-\delta_{2}\int_{\partial M}d^{d}x\sqrt{g_{(0)}}\,\sigma_{1}(x)(\mathcal{L}_{g}+\mathcal{L}_{h})=0\,,

(5.34)

where
g^{(0)}_{ij}
now transforms with the beta-function (
5.29
).
From (
2.149
) we interpret
\mathcal{L}_{g}^{(n)}+\mathcal{L}_{h}^{(n)}
as the trace anomaly of the
d=2n
dimensional CFT at the boundary.

5

As in sec.
2.4.5
, we neglect divergent terms that are cancelled by counterterms.
In
[
HSS01aa
]
the counterterms considered only cancel negative powers of the radial coordinate; with irrelevant operators there is also need of logarithmic counterterms, as considered (in flat space) in
[
Ree11aac
